Anomaly-based detection instead builds a behavioral baseline of what normal traffic looks like – typical connection rates, usual protocols per host, expected working-hour activity – and flags deviations regardless of whether any signature matches. That lets it catch a genuinely novel attack, at the cost of a higher false-positive rate: legitimate but unusual behavior (a 2 a.m. backup job, a one-off mass database query) can trigger it just as easily.

Every detection verdict falls into one of four outcomes, and the vocabulary matters because it is how analysts talk about tuning. A true positive is a real attack correctly caught. A false positive is benign traffic incorrectly flagged – the daily burden that produces alert fatigue. A false negative is a real attack that passed through undetected – the single most dangerous outcome. A true negative is benign traffic correctly left alone. A sensor tuned too sensitively drowns analysts in false positives until they start ignoring alerts altogether, letting real attacks slip past unnoticed. A sensor tuned too loosely produces false negatives directly. This tension, not raw detection capability, is what real IDS/IPS management is actually about.

Sensors also differ in placement. A NIDS (Network IDS) sits at a network choke point and inspects traffic flowing past. It sees an attack before it reaches any host, but cannot inspect encrypted payloads without separately performing TLS inspection. A HIDS (Host IDS) runs as an agent directly on an endpoint, watching system calls, file changes, and process creation. It sees what actually executed, at the cost of needing an agent on every host it protects. Enterprise deployments typically run both together.

Common Misconception

“Signature-based IDS catches all attacks.” Only attacks with an existing, matching signature are caught. A zero-day or a sufficiently novel variant passes through undetected unless anomaly-based detection is also running alongside it.

Network Monitoring and Traffic Analysis

An IDS signature engine catches attacks it already recognizes. Network monitoring is the broader, complementary discipline of continuously watching traffic itself – volume, ports, destinations, timing – well enough to notice when something looks wrong even with no matching signature at all.

Two views of the same traffic serve different purposes. A full packet capture (pcap) records every byte of every packet, precise but expensive to store and slow to search at scale. A flow record (commonly NetFlow) instead summarizes one conversation – source and destination IP, ports, protocol, byte and packet counts, duration – without keeping the payload at all. That makes it far cheaper to store and query, which is why most enterprise monitoring pipelines run on flow data, reserving full capture for a smaller set of high-value segments or triggered deep-dive investigations.

A SIEM (Security Information and Event Management) platform aggregates the alerts and logs these sensors produce – IDS/IPS hits, flow records, endpoint events – into one searchable system. An analyst is not switching between a dozen separate consoles to piece a timeline together. A network detection and response (NDR) platform goes one step further: it layers the anomaly-based monitoring described below directly on top of flow and packet data. It then takes automated response action itself – isolating a host, blocking a flow – collapsing detection and first-line containment into a single product.

Definition: Baselining

Recording typical traffic patterns over time – which hosts talk to which others, on which ports, at what volume, during what hours – to establish what “normal” looks like before anything can be called abnormal.

Several recurring patterns are worth watching regardless of whether any signature exists for them. Unusual port usage – a host suddenly using a port it has never touched before – is worth investigating on its own, since the port itself is the anomaly. Beaconing is malware “phoning home” to a command-and-control server at suspiciously regular, machine-precise intervals – every 60 seconds, every 5 minutes – rather than the irregular bursts typical of human-driven traffic. It is exactly the kind of pattern traffic timing analysis catches without ever inspecting payload content. Data-volume spikes – a host that normally uploads a few megabytes suddenly sending several gigabytes externally, especially outside business hours – are a strong indicator of exfiltration in progress. The signal here is volume and direction alone, not what the data actually contains. Off-baseline timing – an otherwise unremarkable large database query at 3 a.m. from a workstation account – raises suspicion even when every individual packet looks entirely benign.

A firewall enforces which conversations are allowed to start. An IDS matches traffic against known bad patterns. Network monitoring catches what neither can: an attack using entirely permitted ports and protocols, with no matching signature, whose volume, timing, or destination simply deviates from what the network normally does. This is why mature security operations run all three layers together rather than trusting any single one.

Common Misconception

“You need full packet capture to detect an attack.” Most anomalies – volume spikes, beaconing, unusual ports – are visible in flow records alone, without ever inspecting payload content at all.

Incident Response

Detecting an attack, by any of the means above, is only the beginning. What an organization does in the minutes and hours afterward determines whether the incident stays a contained inconvenience or becomes a catastrophic breach.

Definition: Incident-handling lifecycle

The six-phase model popularized by the SANS Institute – often abbreviated PICERL[1]: Preparation, Identification, Containment, Eradication, Recovery, and Lessons Learned, followed strictly in that order so that critical steps are never skipped under the pressure of an active incident. NIST’s own guidance, SP 800-61, groups the identical work into four broader phases instead – Preparation; Detection and Analysis; Containment, Eradication, and Recovery; and Post-Incident Activity[2] – so the two models describe the same underlying process at different granularity, not two competing processes.

Preparation happens entirely before any incident occurs – writing the response plan, defining roles and an escalation chain, deploying the logging and monitoring tools from earlier in this chapter. It also means running tabletop exercises, so the team is not improvising for the first time during a real breach. Identification is where an IDS alert or a traffic-monitoring anomaly actually earns its keep: confirming that an incident is genuinely occurring rather than routine noise, then scoping which systems, data, and timeframe are affected. False positives get filtered out here, before resources commit to a full response.

Containment stops the incident from spreading further, without necessarily fixing the root cause yet – isolating an infected host, disabling a compromised account, blocking a malicious IP at the firewall. Practitioners distinguish short-term containment (an immediate, possibly temporary measure, like unplugging a cable) from long-term containment (a more durable fix that can hold while eradication is planned). Eradication then removes the actual cause – deleting malware, disabling a backdoor account the attacker created, patching the exploited vulnerability.

Why order is not optional

Eradicating before containing is a common and costly mistake. Cleaning malware off a host that remains connected to a live network can tip the attacker off that they have been discovered, or simply allow immediate reinfection from another already-compromised host on the same segment. Containment exists specifically to remove that risk before eradication ever begins. The sequence is not a formality – it is what prevents cleanup from being undone in real time by an attacker who is still watching.

Recovery restores systems to normal operation – rebuilding from clean backups, reconnecting to the network, closely monitoring the restored systems afterward. It only begins once eradication is verified complete: bringing a system back online before its root cause is actually removed just restarts the incident. Lessons Learned closes the loop: documenting a timeline of what happened, evaluating how well the response plan actually worked, and updating that plan, tooling, or training based on what was learned. Skipping this phase is how organizations end up repeating the same incident. The technical fix in Eradication addressed this one instance, but the process gap that allowed it in the first place – a missing patch policy, an under-monitored segment – goes unaddressed and waits for the next attacker to find it again.

Common Misconception

“You should remove the malware as soon as you find it.” Removing malware before containment can tip off the attacker or allow immediate reinfection. Contain first, so the environment is stable, and only then eradicate.

Detection Engineering in Practice

The vocabulary from the previous two sections becomes concrete only once it is written down as a rule a sensor actually evaluates. This section walks through three rule formats an analyst encounters directly. It then covers two small calculations – entropy and timing variance – that turn a raw log file into the indicators described above without ever inspecting a payload.

Common Misconception

“NIST SP 800-94 was updated for the encrypted, cloud-era internet.” The 2007 edition (Scarfone & Mell) remains NIST’s only finalized IDS/IPS guidance.[3] A revision was drafted in 2012 but was later retired without being finalized – NIST itself noted its inputs no longer matched current threat models. The 2007 document’s vocabulary (signature-based, anomaly-based, stateful-protocol-analysis detection; NIDS, HIDS, wireless IDS) is still the field’s canonical terminology. But the guidance predates encrypted-by-default traffic, cloud workloads, and machine-learning-based detection entirely, and must be read with that gap in mind – it was never brought current, only left in place.

A Suricata rule is the canonical format for a signature keyed to packet-level behavior. The following rule flags a single source IP making an unusually high number of connection attempts to distinct destination ports in a short window – a half-open SYN sweep, the classic signature of active reconnaissance (see Chapter 3.1):

Each clause does distinct work. The header (alert tcp $EXTERNAL_NET any -> $HOME_NET any) states the action, protocol, and direction – any external host, any port, to any host and port on the protected network. flags:S,12 matches a bare SYN packet with reserved bits masked, the fingerprint of a half-open scan rather than a normal handshake. The threshold keyword is what makes this a sweep detector rather than a single-packet rule: it fires only once at least 20 matching packets from the same source arrive within 60 seconds. That converts a noisy raw signal into one alert per incident instead of twenty. The sid (rule ID) and rev (revision) fields are bookkeeping required by the engine. reference ties the alert directly to a MITRE ATT&CK technique, so an analyst can look up the adjacent techniques an attacker who reached this stage might attempt next.

Not every detector matches packets directly. Zeek (formerly Bro) instead decodes protocols into structured transaction logs – one line per connection, DNS query, or TLS handshake – and leaves detection to analysis over those records. That lets an analyst ask a question like “which internal hosts negotiated TLS with a rare client fingerprint” instead of “does any packet match this byte pattern.” A short Zeek script expresses exactly that:

Organizations change SIEM platforms far more often than they want to rewrite an entire detection library. Sigma solves that problem: it expresses a log-based detection once, in vendor-neutral YAML, and a converter compiles it into Splunk SPL, Elasticsearch queries, or another platform’s native syntax. The rule below encodes the DNS-tunnelling indicators from the previous section directly:

Two of the indicators introduced earlier – a high-entropy DNS label and a beaconing interval – are recoverable with nothing more than the Python standard library. Shannon entropy measures the unpredictability of a string in bits per character. A label like api is low-entropy (predictable, dictionary-like), while a label carrying base32- or base64-encoded exfiltrated data is close to the maximum entropy its length allows. The coefficient of variation (CV) of a series of connection timestamps – standard deviation divided by the mean gap – measures how machine-regular the intervals are. Malware beaconing every 60 seconds produces a tiny CV, while irregular human browsing produces a large one. Because CV is unitless, a 30-second beacon and a 30-minute beacon land on the same scale.

Run against a real log, shannon_entropy("api") returns about 1.58 bits/char (its own ceiling – three distinct letters, each equally likely). A longer, DGA-style label such as shannon_entropy("4a6f68e9") instead returns close to 4 bits/char, sustained across eight characters instead of three: short strings can hit their ceiling by chance, but sustained high entropy over a longer label cannot. Feeding interval_cv a fixed 30-minute beacon with 6% jitter still returns a CV under 0.1, while ordinary human browsing timestamps return a CV well above 1. The gap is wide enough that a simple fixed threshold, not a machine-learning model, already separates the two cases correctly.

Common Misconception

“Normalizing entropy by a label’s maximum possible entropy gives a cleaner signal.” It does the opposite. A three-letter label like api already sits at its own ceiling (ratio \(1.00\)) purely because it is short, while a 26-character encoded label can score a lower ratio (around \(0.79\)) despite carrying far more actual disorder. Absolute entropy read alongside label length is the correct signal – the normalized ratio actively misleads for short strings.

Check Your Understanding

1. Which of the following is an automated security tool whose primary function is to monitor network traffic and generate an alert when a known attack signature is matched?

A NIDS passively monitors network traffic at a capture point, compares packets against a database of known attack signatures, and raises alerts when matches are found. It is one of the primary automated tools used to detect network attacks. The other options serve credential management, file size reduction, and IP address assignment functions respectively, none of which involve traffic inspection or attack detection.

2. A network administrator wants to monitor all inbound traffic at a corporate gateway and automatically block packets that match known malicious patterns, not just alert on them. Which tool should the administrator deploy?

An IPS sits inline with network traffic and can actively block or drop packets that match attack signatures or exceed anomaly thresholds, it is a preventive control. An IDS is a detective-only control that generates alerts but does not block traffic. A sniffer captures packets for analysis without taking any blocking action. A NAT device translates IP addresses and has no intrusion detection or prevention function.

3. Which of the following correctly identifies the key difference between signature-based and anomaly-based intrusion detection?

Signature-based IDS compares traffic or behavior against a database of pre-written rules for known attacks; it is effective and low in false positives for known threats but blind to novel ones. Anomaly-based IDS learns a baseline of normal behavior and raises alerts for statistical deviations; this allows detection of zero-days but at the cost of more frequent false positives during the baselining period.

Review Questions

  1. A newly discovered exploit has no existing signature in an IDS’s database. Explain why signature-based detection misses it entirely and describe the specific property of anomaly-based detection that could catch it instead, along with the cost that comes with that capability.
  2. An IDS is tuned so sensitively that analysts receive hundreds of alerts per day, most of them benign. Explain, using the TP/FP/FN/TN vocabulary, the specific danger this creates even though the sensor is technically working as configured.
  3. A host that has never used port 4444 begins making regular outbound connections to the same external IP every 60 seconds, each transferring only a few kilobytes. Explain which two monitoring indicators from this chapter this scenario exhibits and why payload inspection is not required to catch it.
  4. An incident responder eradicates malware from a compromised host before isolating it from the network. Explain the specific risk this creates and why containment is sequenced before eradication in the incident-handling lifecycle.
  5. Explain why “Lessons Learned” is treated as a required phase of incident response rather than an optional follow-up, using a concrete example of what goes wrong when it is skipped.
  6. The Sigma rule in this chapter’s DNS-tunnelling example combines a long-label condition with an excluded-domains condition rather than flagging every long DNS label. Explain what specific false-positive source the excluded-domains clause is guarding against, and why omitting it would degrade the rule’s precision without improving its recall.
  7. A 30-minute beacon with 6% timing jitter still produces a coefficient of variation under 0.1, clearly separable from ordinary human browsing. Explain why CV, rather than the raw standard deviation of the intervals, is the right statistic to compare a 30-second beacon against a 30-minute beacon on the same scale.

Answer Key

  1. Signature-based detection matches traffic only against a database of already-catalogued patterns, so an exploit with no existing signature simply matches nothing and passes through undetected by construction, regardless of how obviously malicious its behavior actually is. Anomaly-based detection instead builds a behavioral baseline of normal activity and flags any significant deviation from it. That lets it catch a genuinely novel attack with no signature at all – at the cost of a higher false-positive rate, since legitimate but unusual behavior can deviate from the baseline just as sharply as an actual attack does. 3.5.C
  2. Hundreds of daily alerts, most of them false positives, produce alert fatigue: analysts begin tuning out or triaging alerts less carefully simply to keep pace with the volume. The specific danger is that this raises the effective false-negative rate even though the sensor itself is technically functioning as configured. A real attack (a true positive) can arrive buried among the noise and be dismissed or missed entirely by an exhausted analyst – a failure of the human response process sitting downstream of a technically correct detection. 3.5.D
  3. This scenario exhibits unusual port usage (a host using port 4444, which it has never touched before) and beaconing (regular, machine-precise 60-second intervals to the same external IP, transferring a near-constant small volume each time). That interval pattern is exactly the coefficient-of-variation signature distinguishing malware beaconing from irregular human-driven traffic. Payload inspection is not required because both indicators live entirely in metadata – which port is used, how regular the timing is, how much data moves – all of which is visible in flow records even when the payload itself is fully encrypted. 3.5.E
  4. Eradicating malware from a host that remains connected to the network risks tipping off the attacker that they have been discovered, prompting them to escalate or destroy evidence. It can also allow immediate reinfection from another already-compromised host on the same segment that was never isolated. Containment is sequenced before eradication in this lifecycle specifically to remove that risk first – stabilizing the environment so that cleanup performed during eradication cannot be undone in real time by an attacker who is still actively watching or still has other footholds available. 3.5.A
  5. Lessons Learned is required, not optional, because Eradication only fixes the specific technical instance of an incident – the malware, the exploited vulnerability – while leaving unaddressed whatever process gap allowed the incident to happen in the first place, such as a missing patch policy or an under-monitored network segment. If this phase is skipped, the organization documents no timeline, evaluates nothing about how well the response plan performed, and updates neither tooling nor training. That is precisely how the same category of incident recurs: the next attacker finds the identical unaddressed gap the first one exploited. 3.5.B
  6. Content-delivery and reverse-DNS infrastructure legitimately generates long, high-entropy- looking subdomain labels as a normal part of how those services operate – a CDN edge node or a reverse-lookup zone is not evidence of tunnelling. The excluded-domains clause filters out exactly this known-benign source before the count threshold is evaluated. Omitting it would not catch any additional real tunnelling traffic that the long-label condition was not already going to catch. It would only add legitimate CDN and reverse-DNS traffic to the alert population, which lowers precision (more false positives) while leaving recall (the fraction of real tunnelling caught) completely unchanged. 3.5.E
  7. Standard deviation alone is expressed in the same units as the interval itself, so a 30-second beacon’s jitter and a 30-minute beacon’s jitter produce very different raw standard-deviation numbers even at the identical 6% relative regularity. That makes the two incomparable on one fixed threshold. Dividing by the mean gap (producing CV) normalizes away the absolute timescale, so a beacon’s relative regularity – how small its jitter is compared to its own interval – is what gets compared. Both a 30-second and a 30-minute beacon at the same relative regularity land on the same CV scale, which is exactly the property that lets one fixed threshold work regardless of the beacon’s period. 3.5.E

Sources

  1. [1] Patrick Kral, The Incident Handler’s Handbook, SANS Institute InfoSec Reading Room, 2011 – the widely-taught source for the six-phase PICERL (Preparation, Identification, Containment, Eradication, Recovery, Lessons Learned) incident-handling model.
  2. [2] Paul Cichonski, Tom Millar, Tim Grance, and Karen Scarfone, Computer Security Incident Handling Guide, NIST Special Publication 800-61 Revision 2, National Institute of Standards and Technology, August 2012.
  3. [3] Karen Scarfone and Peter Mell, Guide to Intrusion Detection and Prevention Systems (IDPS), NIST Special Publication 800-94, National Institute of Standards and Technology, February 2007; Draft NIST SP 800-94 Revision 1 (July 2012) was never finalized and was formally retired in 2022.

Unit 4: Securing Devices

Unit 3 secured the network the devices talk over. Unit 4 secures the devices themselves, on the premise that a perfectly locked-down network still fails if the laptop connected to it is running outdated software with a known, unpatched vulnerability. Chapter 4.1 surveys where those vulnerabilities actually come from – unpatched operating systems, insecure default configurations, outdated firmware – and why an endpoint is often the weakest link precisely because it is the device a user controls directly.

Chapter 4.2 takes on the first line of defense for any device: authentication. This chapter goes past “use a strong password” into what actually makes multi-factor authentication resistant to the credential-theft techniques covered back in Unit 1, and where it can still be defeated. Chapter 4.3 broadens out to endpoint hardening generally – disabling unused services, enforcing least privilege on the device itself, keeping software patched – the device-level version of the access-control philosophy introduced in Chapter 2.3.

Chapter 4.4 closes the unit, again, with auditing: how an organization confirms its endpoints are actually configured the way policy says they should be, rather than assuming compliance and finding out otherwise during an incident. By now the prevent-control-detect rhythm from the last two units should feel automatic, which is the point – it is the same framework, one layer deeper.

4.1 Device Endpoint Vulnerabilities

Only amateurs attack machines; professionals target people.

Bruce Schneier, “Semantic Attacks: The Third Wave of Network Attacks” (2000)

Motivation

Units 2 and 3 protected space and wire. Unit 4 moves to the endpoint itself – the laptop, phone, camera, or thermostat where data actually lives and computation actually happens. This chapter covers three distinct ways an endpoint gets compromised: malicious software running on it, and physical tampering with the hardware itself. It also covers the enormous, largely undefended category of Internet of Things devices, which fail in both of the first two ways simultaneously, at a scale no other device category matches.

AP Course Alignment

Unit 4: Securing Devices · Topic 4.1 – Device Vulnerabilities and Attacks

  • 4.1.A Analyze Risk Recognize the different classes of computing device – servers, workstations and laptops, mobile handsets, network appliances, and embedded/industrial controllers with no screen or keyboard – and account for how each class differs in what it can run, what it can be patched with, and how long its vendor will support it.
  • 4.1.B Analyze Risk Work out, from what an incident actually did, which family of malicious software was involved: whether the code needed a host file and a human, whether it moved between machines unaided, whether it arrived disguised as legitimate software, and whether its purpose was extortion, concealment, surveillance, or theft.
  • 4.1.C Analyze Risk Trace the route from a weakness in a device – a factory password, an unneeded service, an unpatched kernel, unsupported firmware – to the concrete harm an adversary can produce with it: data lost, hardware damaged, operations halted, records destroyed.
  • 4.1.D Analyze Risk Evaluate the weaknesses present on a fleet of devices and record them in a form someone else can act on – each finding scored for likelihood and impact, ranked against the others, and documented where the decision to fix or accept it can later be audited.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

Device Classes and the Support Lifecycle

Not every endpoint is the same kind of target. Five device classes recur across a typical organization, and each one differs in what it can run, how it gets patched, and how long its vendor keeps patching it at all.

  • Servers run general-purpose operating systems and patch on a scheduled, change-controlled basis – downtime costs money, so a patch waits for a maintenance window rather than shipping the moment a fix exists.
  • Workstations and laptops run the same kind of general-purpose operating system but patch far more aggressively, often automatically, because a compromised end-user machine is usually the first hop into the rest of the network.
  • Mobile handsets run a locked-down operating system controlled by one vendor, which pushes security updates directly to the device. That vendor also sets its own support cutoff, though: past that date, even a healthy phone stops receiving patches entirely.
  • Network appliances – routers, firewalls, switches – run stripped-down firmware built for one function. An administrator updates it rarely, and only by scheduling an outage.
  • Embedded and industrial controllers have no screen, no keyboard, and often no practical way to patch at all. The firmware that shipped with the device may be the only firmware it ever runs, across a service life measured in decades rather than years.

The pattern across all five classes is the same: the harder a device is to patch, and the longer it stays in service, the longer any given vulnerability in it stays exploitable. An unpatched laptop is a bad week. An unpatched industrial controller can be a bad decade.

Malware Types

Definition: Malware

Malicious software: any program intentionally designed to cause harm, steal data, disrupt operations, or give an attacker unauthorized control.

Four categories account for the overwhelming majority of real-world device compromises. Each has a genuinely different mechanism, which is exactly why classifying an infection correctly matters – the right defense is specific to the category, not generic.

A virus attaches its code to a legitimate host file – an executable, a macro-enabled document, a script – and activates only when a user runs that host file. At that point it typically searches for other files to infect, and spreads further whenever an infected file is copied or emailed elsewhere. Its defining trait is dependence on a host file and direct user action to propagate. A worm shares the self-replicating instinct but needs neither: it is a self-contained program that actively probes a network and finds vulnerable devices. It copies itself across automatically, repeating the process on each new host, with no user interaction required anywhere in the chain. The 2003 SQL Slammer worm shows how fast that unaided spread can go: it doubled its infected-host count every 8.5 seconds by exploiting a single buffer-overflow flaw in Microsoft SQL Server. Within its first ten minutes it reached an estimated 75,000 vulnerable hosts.[1]

A trojan takes the opposite approach entirely: it does not self-replicate at all, relying instead on disguise – presenting itself as a free game, a pirated application, a “system cleaner” – so the user installs it willingly. Once installed, a trojan often opens a back door granting an attacker remote control, keystroke logging, or a launch point for further attacks. And because the user installed it themselves, tools that look for unauthorized changes may never flag the initial infection at all. Ransomware encrypts a victim’s files with a key only the attacker holds, then demands payment, typically in cryptocurrency, for the decryption key. It disproportionately targets hospitals, schools, and municipalities, precisely because they cannot afford the downtime that refusing to pay would cost them.

These categories are not mutually exclusive in a real attack – a worm might carry a trojan payload that later downloads ransomware, stacking three mechanisms into one incident. Malware enters through an infection vector: a phishing attachment (Chapter 1.1), a drive-by download, an infected USB drive, or exploitation of an unpatched vulnerability. Because the defense is vector-specific, recognizing which vector was used is not academic. No attachment-scanning policy stops worm spread; no network segmentation stops a user from installing a disguised trojan; only an offline backup neutralizes ransomware’s actual leverage, since it removes the “pay or lose everything” choice entirely.

Common Misconception

“Ransomware destroys your files.” Ransomware typically encrypts files, leaving them fully intact but inaccessible without the key – the attacker can decrypt them if paid, which is exactly the leverage a backup breaks. The files are locked, not gone.

Device Tampering

The most fundamental rule of hardware security is blunt: if an attacker has physical access to your device, it is no longer your device. Passwords, firewalls, and network access lists all assume the physical chassis itself is secure. Once that assumption fails, almost every software-based defense can be bypassed without ever touching the operating system’s login prompt.

A USB Rubber Ducky exploits the fact that operating systems automatically trust a keyboard the instant one is plugged in. The device is programmed to mimic a USB keyboard rather than storage, and once connected it types scripted commands at over 1,000 words per minute[2] – opening a terminal, downloading malware, exfiltrating data. None of it looks like a file to scan, so antivirus never sees it. A hardware keylogger sits inline between the keyboard cable and the computer, intercepting every electrical signal before it ever reaches the operating system. That is precisely why no software-based scanner can detect it: the interception happens entirely in hardware, one layer below anything software can observe.

The cold boot attack

When a computer powers off, its RAM does not lose its contents instantly – it degrades over several seconds to minutes. An attacker who sprays the memory chips with compressed-air duster held upside down – so it discharges a cold liquid instead of a gas – can chill them to roughly \(-50^\circ\)C, freezing that decay in place. At that temperature, research on the technique found that cutting power for a full minute still let an attacker recover at least 99.9% of the RAM’s bits.[3] The attacker then quickly transfers the physical RAM module to another machine before it fades, extracting cryptographic keys or passwords that were resident in memory moments before. The attack works entirely on physics the operating system has no way to prevent: by the time it runs, the OS that would have protected that memory is no longer even executing.

Physical attacks succeed because operating systems are built for convenience first. They trust a newly connected keyboard implicitly, with no administrator privilege required. By default they will also boot from whatever storage device is connected – letting a live Linux USB override the installed OS and every protection running inside it. Full-disk encryption (FDE) defeats this specific path. Bound to a Trusted Platform Module (TPM) that verifies boot integrity before releasing the decryption key, FDE ensures that an attacker who removes the drive or boots from external media still cannot read the encrypted contents without the key. BIOS/UEFI password protection and disabling external-media boot close the boot-integrity gap directly, while port blockers, cable locks, and chassis intrusion switches raise the physical bar for opportunistic tampering.

Common Misconception

“Operating system passwords protect my data if my laptop is stolen.” A login password only guards the intended boot path. An attacker can remove the drive and read it on another machine, or boot a Linux USB and browse the filesystem directly, unless the disk’s contents are themselves encrypted through FDE.

IoT Device Vulnerabilities

The Internet of Things – cameras, routers, thermostats, door locks, medical sensors, industrial controllers – numbers in the tens of billions of deployed devices, and collectively they are the softest target on the modern network. The reasons recur across nearly every device category, which is what makes them worth understanding as a pattern rather than a checklist.

Most IoT devices ship with default credentials – admin/admin, root/12345, or nothing at all – meant only for initial setup. Most owners never change them, because a smart light bulb does not present as “a computer that needs a password.” Automated scanners try these well-known lists against every IP address on the internet and succeed within seconds wherever the default was never touched. Firmware – the low-level software controlling a device’s core operation – typically has no automatic update mechanism. Many manufacturers stop supporting a device (it becomes end-of-life) within a year or two. Any undiscovered vulnerability then stays exploitable for the rest of the device’s operational life – often five to ten years for a security camera or industrial sensor.

Devices frequently expose management interfaces over old, insecure protocols to keep setup convenient. Telnet, a plaintext remote-login protocol, is one; UPnP is another, letting a device silently punch a hole through a home router’s firewall without the owner’s knowledge and exposing an administrative interface directly to the internet. Their companion mobile apps depend on cloud APIs that, if improperly authenticated, let an attacker control or query devices belonging to other users entirely. Traffic sent without TLS, meanwhile, is readable by anyone sharing the same network segment. Physical accessibility compounds all of this: a doorbell camera on an exterior wall or a sensor in a parking garage is reachable by anyone who can touch it. That often lets an attacker extract firmware directly from a chip or attach a debug interface, bypassing network defenses entirely.

Mirai and the aggregate risk of scale

Any single insecure smart plug looks low-stakes in isolation. The 2016 Mirai botnet showed why scale changes that calculus completely. It scanned the internet for IoT devices still using factory-default Telnet credentials and logged in automatically wherever it found one. That enlisted each device into a network that reached over 600,000 compromised devices under central control at its peak.[4] Mirai used that aggregate to launch some of the largest distributed-denial-of-service attacks ever recorded, including the October 2016 attack on DNS provider Dyn that briefly knocked Twitter, Netflix, and other major sites offline. No individual device mattered; the aggregate was devastating. That is exactly why security teams increasingly worry about shadow IoT – devices connected to a network that IT never approved or secured, silently expanding the attack surface with nobody tracking it.

Common Misconception

“If a device isn’t connected to the internet, it’s safe.” A device on a local network can still be reached by anything else already on that network – a compromised laptop, a malicious guest device – and many “local-only” devices still phone home to a cloud API regardless. Network placement reduces risk; it does not eliminate it.

Check Your Understanding

1. A security team investigates an internet-connected tractor after its autopilot begins moving to unauthorized GPS coordinates. Forensic review reveals hidden software that silently collects location data and transmits it to an external server at regular intervals. Which type of malware best describes this threat?

Spyware is malware designed to covertly monitor and exfiltrate data, here, GPS telemetry, without user awareness or consent. Ransomware encrypts data for extortion; a worm self-replicates across a network; a logic bomb triggers on a specific condition (e.g., a date or event) rather than running continuously. Only spyware matches the persistent, silent data-collection behavior described.

2. An automated milking machine's onboard computer is observed making periodic outbound connections to a cluster of unfamiliar IP addresses, transmitting small data packets at regular intervals even when idle. No legitimate application accounts for this traffic. Which malware classification most accurately describes this behavior?

A bot (botnet node) is a compromised device controlled remotely by a command-and-control server, exhibiting periodic beacon traffic to C2 infrastructure, exactly what is described. A rootkit hides malicious activity rather than generating periodic C2 traffic. A trojan is defined by its deceptive delivery method, not its ongoing behavior. Adware generates advertising-related traffic, not command-and-control beacons.

3. Following a cyberattack, an irrigation controller's stored configuration files are encrypted and access is blocked. A message appears on the management dashboard reading: "Your device data has been locked. Send 0.5 BTC to [address] within 72 hours to recover your files." Which malware type was used in this attack?

Ransomware encrypts a victim's data and demands payment (usually cryptocurrency) for the decryption key. All three defining elements are present: encryption of files, loss of access, and an explicit ransom demand. Spyware exfiltrates data quietly. A keylogger records keystrokes. A backdoor provides persistent access but does not inherently encrypt data or demand payment.

Review Questions

  1. A hospital patches its laptop fleet automatically within days of every vendor security bulletin, but its infusion-pump controllers are still running the firmware they shipped with a decade ago. Explain why these two device classes end up with such different exposure windows, referencing what each class can run and how (or whether) it gets patched.
  2. A university network shows thousands of new infections within minutes, with no phishing email or user download involved in any of them. Identify which malware category this matches and explain the specific mechanical property that makes this scale of spread possible without user action.
  3. Explain why antivirus software cannot detect a hardware keylogger, tracing the explanation to exactly where in the system the interception occurs relative to the operating system.
  4. A stolen laptop has a strong login password but no full-disk encryption. Explain precisely how an attacker extracts its data anyway, and identify the single control that would have prevented it.
  5. An IoT device has default credentials, outdated firmware, and unencrypted traffic simultaneously. Using the priority-ordered audit logic from this chapter, explain which weakness should be reported first and why the ordering itself is a meaningful design decision, not arbitrary.
  6. Explain why the Mirai botnet is described as an aggregate risk rather than an individual-device risk, and explain what specific IoT weakness it exploited to reach its scale.
  7. Using the malware taxonomy diagram, explain why “ransomware” and “trojan” are not mutually exclusive categories the way “virus” and “worm” are, and describe a realistic infection chain that involves both labels correctly at once.
  8. A security team has a strong attachment-scanning policy and a strong network-segmentation policy but no offline backups. Using the vector-specific reasoning from this chapter, explain which malware category this fleet remains fully exposed to and why neither existing control addresses it.

Answer Key

  1. Workstations and laptops run general-purpose operating systems with vendor-managed, often automatic patch delivery, so a newly disclosed vulnerability’s exposure window is measured in days. Embedded and industrial controllers such as infusion pumps run purpose-built firmware with no screen or keyboard, often no practical update mechanism at all, and a service life measured in decades rather than years – so a vulnerability discovered once can stay exploitable for the device’s entire remaining life. The gap is not about which device matters more; it is about what each device class can run, and whether that software can realistically be replaced after the device is deployed. 4.1.A
  2. The infection matches a worm. A worm is a self-contained program that actively probes a network for vulnerable hosts and copies itself across automatically, with no host file and no user action required anywhere in the chain – which is exactly the mechanical property that lets an infection count climb into the thousands within minutes, the same doubling behavior the 2003 Slammer worm showed. 4.1.B
  3. A hardware keylogger sits inline on the physical cable between the keyboard and the computer, intercepting the electrical keystroke signal before it ever reaches the operating system. Antivirus software runs as code within the operating system, one layer above where the interception happens, so it has no vantage point from which the tampering is even visible. 4.1.C
  4. A login password only protects the intended boot path. The attacker can simply remove the drive and read it on another machine, or boot the laptop from a live Linux USB and browse the filesystem directly. Either path bypasses the login prompt entirely, because the data on disk was never encrypted. Full-disk encryption (FDE) is the single control that would have prevented this, since it makes the drive’s contents unreadable without the key regardless of which machine reads the physical disk. 4.1.C
  5. Default credentials should be reported first. The audit logic in this chapter checks it first because it is both the easiest weakness to exploit (no skill or exploit code needed, only a known username/password pair) and the one automated internet-wide scanners actively search for at scale, whereas outdated firmware or unencrypted traffic both require more attacker effort to leverage. The ordering reflects likelihood of exploitation, not an arbitrary list position. 4.1.D
  6. Mirai is an aggregate risk because no single insecure smart plug or camera caused meaningful harm on its own; the damage came from combining hundreds of thousands of such devices under one attacker’s control to launch massive distributed denial-of-service attacks. It exploited factory default Telnet credentials that device owners never changed, letting Mirai’s scanner log in automatically at internet scale. 4.1.B
  7. Virus and worm differ on a spreading mechanism – host-file-and-user versus self-contained – so an infection is mechanically one or the other, never both at once. Ransomware and trojan differ on a different axis entirely: trojan describes how a program gets installed (disguise rather than self-replication), while ransomware describes what the payload does once running (encrypt files and demand payment). A realistic chain: a user downloads a disguised “free software cracker” (a trojan, by installation method) which, once run, encrypts the user’s files and displays a ransom note (ransomware, by payload behavior) – both labels correctly describe the same single infection because they answer different questions about it. 4.1.B
  8. The fleet remains fully exposed to ransomware. Attachment scanning targets the phishing infection vector and network segmentation limits lateral movement after a compromise, but neither addresses ransomware’s actual leverage, which is the absence of any way to recover encrypted files without paying. A well-disguised trojan can still be willingly installed by a user despite attachment scanning (if it arrives by a different vector, such as a fake software download), and once ransomware executes locally it does not need to move laterally to cause damage – only offline backups remove the “pay or lose everything” choice that is ransomware’s entire mechanism of harm. 4.1.B

Sources

  1. [1] David Moore, Vern Paxson, Stuart Staniford, et al., “The Spread of the Sapphire/Slammer Worm,” CAIDA (Cooperative Association for Internet Data Analysis), 2003.
  2. [2] Hak5, “USB Rubber Ducky” product specifications, shop.hak5.org (accessed 2026).
  3. [3] J. Alex Halderman, Seth D. Schoen, Nadia Heninger, et al., “Lest We Remember: Cold-Boot Attacks on Encryption Keys,” Communications of the ACM, vol. 52, no. 5 (May 2009).
  4. [4] Elie Bursztein, “Inside Mirai the Infamous IoT Botnet: A Retrospective Analysis,” elie.net, 2017; peak device count corroborated by contemporaneous reporting on the October 2016 Dyn DNS attack.

4.2 Device Authentication Hardening

Treat your password like your toothbrush. Don’t let anybody else use it, and get a new one every six months.

commonly attributed to Clifford Stoll

Motivation

Chapter 4.1 covered how an endpoint gets compromised. This chapter covers the layer that decides who is allowed to touch it in the first place, and what happens after they successfully do. Four topics build on each other in sequence. First, the password itself. Second, the factor that backs it up when it inevitably leaks. Third, the session that keeps a login active without re-asking for a password on every click. Fourth, the access-control model that decides how much a successfully authenticated account is actually allowed to do. Each layer assumes the one before it can fail.

AP Course Alignment

Unit 4: Securing Devices · Topic 4.2 – Authentication

  • 4.2.A Analyze Risk Account for why a system keeps the output of a one-way function – a hash, digest, or checksum – in place of the password itself, and what that substitution does and does not buy when the credential store is stolen.
  • 4.2.B Analyze Risk Explain the mechanism behind each family of attack on credentials – exhaustive search, wordlists, precomputed tables, reused breach data, and low-and-slow guessing across many accounts – identifying which weakness each one feeds on and which single defense removes it.
  • 4.2.C Mitigate Risk Given a description of how a person proved who they were, determine which categories of evidence were actually used, whether the combination constitutes more than one independent factor, and how resistant the resulting scheme is to interception.
  • 4.2.D Mitigate Risk Distinguish session fixation from session hijacking by when in the login lifecycle each attack plants or steals its token, and identify which session control – regeneration, the Secure/HttpOnly/SameSite cookie flags, or timeout policy – defeats which one.
  • 4.2.E Mitigate Risk Choose an access-control model (DAC, MAC, RBAC, or ABAC) appropriate to a given scenario, and identify the organizational process that catches privilege that accumulates even after an account was originally provisioned correctly.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

Passwords and Password Policy

A password is the oldest authentication mechanism there is: a shared secret known only to the user and the system. Two properties dominate its strength – length and unpredictability – and they do not trade off equally. A brute-force attack tries every possible combination; a dictionary attack tries words, names, and common substitutions. Both become exponentially more expensive as length increases. Adding one character to a ten-character password drawn from a 95-character alphabet multiplies the search space by 95; adding four characters multiplies it by roughly 81 million. Complexity rules – mandatory uppercase, digits, symbols – were introduced to push users off dictionary words, but they are not a substitute for length. A long random passphrase beats a short, symbol-sprinkled word against every attack class, because a predictable substitution pattern (p@ssw0rd) generates almost no additional entropy.

Definition: Password entropy

The number of bits of uncertainty in a password, computed as \(H = L \times \log_2(R)\), where \(L\) is the password’s length in characters and \(R\) is the size of the character set (alphabet) each character is independently drawn from. Entropy measures search-space size, not memorability – a password can be long and still low-entropy if \(R\) is small or the characters are not independently chosen.

Computing entropy for two eight-character passwords

Take Tr0ub4dor&3-style complexity rules versus a long random passphrase, both compared at equal length first. An eight-character password drawn from a 95-character keyboard alphabet (lowercase, uppercase, digits, symbols) has

\[H = 8 \times \log_2(95) \approx 8 \times 6.57 \approx 52.6 \text{ bits.}\]

Compare a four-word passphrase such as correct horse battery staple drawn from a 7,776-word list (the standard Diceware wordlist, so \(R = 7{,}776\) and \(L = 4\) words rather than characters):

\[H = 4 \times \log_2(7{,}776) \approx 4 \times 12.9 \approx 51.5 \text{ bits.}\]

The two land at nearly identical entropy despite the passphrase requiring no memorized symbol substitutions at all. Extending the passphrase to six words pushes it past 77 bits, an increase no realistic complexity rule on an eight-character password can match. The reason is that \(R\) for a human-chosen “complex” password is rarely the full 95-character alphabet in practice: people overwhelmingly reuse the same handful of substitution patterns (a\(\to\)@, s\(\to\)$). An attacker’s cracking dictionary already encodes those patterns, collapsing the effective \(R\) back down toward a much smaller number than the nominal keyboard size suggests.

Definition: NIST SP 800-63B

The 2017 federal digital-identity guideline that reshaped modern password policy: it recommends a minimum length (8 characters, systems must allow at least 64), checking candidates against known breach datasets rather than imposing arbitrary complexity rules, and eliminating forced periodic rotation absent evidence of compromise.[1]

Forced rotation, in particular, backfires in a specific, well-documented way: users cycle through predictable families – Summer2024!, then Fall2024! – that are barely different from each other and just as guessable as the original. Account lockout after a threshold of failures (typically 3–10) closes off online guessing specifically, since an attacker who needs millions of tries cannot make them interactively. The lockout duration itself is a tuning problem, though: too short, and scripted slow-guessing simply waits it out; too long or indefinite, and an attacker can weaponize the lockout itself as a denial-of-service against legitimate users. Password history rejects a candidate matching the last several previous passwords, closing off the “cycle between two passwords” loophole that a naive rotation policy invites.

Two further attack families exploit scale rather than repeated guessing against one account. Credential stuffing takes real username/password pairs leaked from an unrelated breach and replays them against many other sites, betting that some fraction of users reused the same password everywhere. Screening a new password against a known-breach list (the same NIST-recommended control described above) blocks this only at the moment a password is chosen; the complementary defense is watching logins for pairs already known from public breach corpora and forcing a reset on a match. Password spraying inverts the usual lockout assumption: instead of trying many passwords against one account, it tries one or two common passwords against thousands of accounts. It stays under each individual account’s lockout threshold by never repeating a guess on the same one. Per-account lockout does nothing against it – the defense has to watch the account population as a whole, rate-limiting failed logins by source or in aggregate rather than per account.

Storage matters as much as the policy governing creation. Passwords must never be stored in plaintext; the correct mechanism is a salted cryptographic hash using a deliberately slow algorithm – bcrypt, scrypt, or Argon2. The salt, a unique random value prepended before hashing, defeats precomputed rainbow tables, and the algorithm’s slowness makes each guess computationally expensive even after a full database theft. MD5 and SHA-1 are unacceptable for this purpose specifically because they are fast general-purpose hashes – an attacker with a GPU can test billions of candidates per second against them.

The 2012 LinkedIn breach

In June 2012, an attacker posted roughly 6.5 million password hashes stolen from LinkedIn to a Russian hacking forum. LinkedIn never officially disclosed the attack vector, though court filings from the resulting class-action settlement pointed to SQL injection. It later emerged in 2016 that the same theft had actually covered 117 million accounts.[2] LinkedIn’s storage combined two independent failures at once: the hashes were computed with unsalted SHA-1, and no deliberately slow key-stretching step (bcrypt, scrypt, or repeated hash iteration) was applied on top of it. SHA-1 alone is a fast general-purpose hash, so the missing salt let attackers reuse precomputed rainbow tables across every user at once, and the missing key stretching meant each individual guess cost a GPU almost nothing to test. Security researchers cracked more than 60% of the exposed hashes within a single day of the leak, and the fraction climbed further in the weeks that followed.[2] Salting alone would have defeated the rainbow-table shortcut; key stretching alone would have made each guess expensive; LinkedIn had neither, which is why the breach is the textbook illustration of why both properties must hold simultaneously, not just one.

Common Misconception

“Changing your password every 90 days improves security.” Forced rotation produces predictable families that are trivially related to each other. NIST’s current guidance recommends rotating only when there is actual evidence of compromise, not on a fixed calendar.

Multi-Factor Authentication

A password is a single piece of evidence; once an attacker learns it, through phishing, a breach, or a guess, nothing else stops them from logging in as the real user.

Definition: Multi-factor authentication (MFA)

Requiring proof from two or more independent factor categories before granting access: something you know (a password), something you have (a phone, a hardware key), and something you are (a fingerprint, a face scan).

The categories matter more than the count – entering two different passwords is still “something you know” twice, not true MFA, no matter how much it feels like an extra step. Methods vary sharply in how resistant they are to interception. SMS codes are better than a password alone, but the phone number is not tightly bound to the phone itself. A SIM swap tricks or bribes a carrier into porting the victim’s number to attacker-controlled hardware, after which every SMS code arrives on the attacker’s device instead. TOTP authenticator codes remove the SIM-swap risk entirely, since the shared secret lives only on the device. The code can still be phished and relayed in real time by an adversary-in-the-middle kit, though, because the user is still capable of typing it into a convincing fake page. Push notifications are convenient but introduce MFA fatigue (push bombing): an attacker who already holds the password triggers repeated login attempts until an annoyed or confused user taps “Approve” just to make the notifications stop.

Definition: FIDO2 / WebAuthn

An open standard for hardware or platform security keys that cryptographically bind an authentication response to the exact requesting website domain. This makes the resulting handshake phishing-resistant: the key simply will not respond to a domain that does not match, no matter how convincing the fake page looks to a human.

Change Healthcare, February 2024: One Portal, No MFA

Change Healthcare processes a large share of prescription and medical-claims transactions across the U.S. healthcare system. On February 12, 2024, attackers affiliated with the ALPHV/BlackCat ransomware operation reached one of its Citrix remote-access portals using a single stolen username and password – nothing more.[3] UnitedHealth Group’s own CEO later confirmed to Congress that the portal had no multi-factor authentication enabled at all: a leaked credential alone was sufficient to reach it. Once inside, the attackers moved laterally for about nine days before deploying ransomware on February 21. The resulting outage disrupted pharmacy and claims processing nationwide for weeks. UnitedHealth Group’s own disclosures put the direct response and remediation cost at well over $2 billion by the end of 2024.[3] The vulnerability here was not a zero-day or a novel technique. It was the exact gap this section opened with: a password is a single piece of evidence. Once an attacker has it through phishing, a breach, or reuse, nothing else stops them from logging in as the real user, unless a second, independent factor is actually required. One unprotected remote-access portal was enough to cascade into a nationwide outage. That is why MFA enrollment is treated in this chapter as a baseline configuration setting, not an optional hardening step reserved for especially sensitive systems.

Evaluating whether a login satisfies real MFA reduces to two questions. First: do the presented factors come from two or more genuinely different categories? Second, if so: is at least one of them resistant to interception, or does the scheme rely on something relayable, SIM-swappable, or rubber-stampable by an exhausted user?

Common Misconception

“Approving a push notification always means the real user is logging in.” MFA fatigue attacks exploit exactly the fact that a user may approve a prompt they never initiated, purely to stop repeated notifications. Approval is not proof of intent unless the prompt itself shows context the user actually verifies.

Session Security

Authentication happens once; staying logged in across many requests without retyping a password every time is what a session exists to solve.

Definition: Session token (session cookie)

A long, random, unpredictable string a server issues at successful login, which the browser resends with every subsequent request; the server treats any request carrying a valid token as coming from the authenticated user it belongs to.

That convenience has a cost: the token becomes as valuable as the password itself, since whoever holds a valid token is that user as far as the server can tell. Session hijacking is the theft and reuse of an already-authenticated token – through cross-site scripting reading it directly from the page, through sniffing unencrypted HTTP traffic on a shared network, or through malware reading browser storage on the victim’s own device. It requires no knowledge of the victim’s password at all. Session fixation works differently: instead of stealing an existing token, the attacker forces the victim to authenticate under a token the attacker already knows, for instance by sending a link containing a pre-set session identifier. If the server never changes that ID at login, the attacker’s known ID becomes attached to a real authenticated session the moment the victim logs in.

Why the defenses for hijacking and fixation differ

Fixation plants a known ID before login; hijacking steals a live ID after the victim is already authenticated. Session regeneration – issuing a brand-new, freshly random session ID at the moment of login and again at any privilege change – defeats fixation completely, since any ID an attacker planted beforehand is simply discarded and never becomes authenticated. It does nothing for hijacking, though, which instead requires the token itself to be hard to steal. The Secure cookie flag restricts transmission to HTTPS; the HttpOnly flag hides the cookie from JavaScript entirely, so a successful XSS injection still cannot read it; and SameSite restricts whether the cookie rides along with cross-site requests at all.

Expiration limits how much damage any stolen or fixated token can do, regardless of which theft path was used. An idle timeout ends a session after a period of inactivity, protecting a user who walks away from a logged-in device. An absolute timeout ends it after a fixed total duration regardless of activity, limiting how long a stolen token stays useful even if an attacker keeps it alive with periodic requests. Logout must be a server-side action for any of this to matter. A “logout” that only deletes the browser’s cookie leaves the session record alive on the server, so a token an attacker captured earlier keeps working indefinitely – the user’s click on “logout” buys no real protection at all.

Common Misconception

“HTTPS alone makes sessions un-hijackable.” HTTPS protects the token in transit, but a cookie missing the HttpOnly flag can still be read directly out of the page’s JavaScript context by a successful XSS injection, with no network sniffing required at all.

Least Privilege and Access Control Models

Once an account is authenticated, a separate question remains: how much should it actually be allowed to do?

Definition: Principle of least privilege (PoLP)

Every subject – account, process, or service – should be granted the minimum access required to perform its function, and nothing more.

Extra access does not sit harmlessly unused; it expands the blast radius of everything that can go wrong with that account. A phished credential with read-only access to one shared drive exposes exactly that drive; the same credential with write access to a production database exposes the database too, through the identical phishing email. This compounds across three separate threats that share one root cause. External compromise inherits whatever privileges the stolen account holds; insider misuse is bounded by whatever access the insider actually has; malware executing under a user’s session inherits that user’s privileges outright. An over-privileged account that opens one infected attachment can hand ransomware write access to systems far beyond its own workstation.

Organizations implement least privilege through a formal access-control model. DAC (Discretionary Access Control) lets a resource’s owner decide who else can access it – standard Unix file permissions are the textbook case. It is flexible but prone to sprawl, since no central authority enforces consistency as individual owners each grant access independently. MAC (Mandatory Access Control) moves that decision to a central authority using fixed classification labels on both subjects and resources, so a user cannot grant access above their own clearance even if they wanted to. It is common in government and military systems: rigid, but strong against insider misconfiguration specifically because no individual can override it. RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) attaches permissions to roles rather than individuals, so a job change means reassigning a role rather than hand-editing dozens of individual grants – the most common model in enterprise IT because it scales. ABAC (Attribute-Based Access Control) decides access dynamically at request time from attributes of the user, resource, and environment – “allow only if department is finance and the time is business hours and the device is company-managed.” It is more flexible than RBAC because policy reacts to context, at the cost of being harder to audit.

Privilege creep

An employee transfers from Sales to Engineering and, as part of onboarding, receives new system access for the Engineering role. The old Sales access is routinely never revoked, because removing access requires someone to notice and act, while granting new access is a standard onboarding step that happens automatically. This silent accumulation – privilege creep – undermines least privilege even in an organization that started out perfectly configured. That is exactly why a periodic access review exists: on a fixed schedule, every access grant a user holds must be explicitly re-approved, and anything not re-approved is revoked outright.

Least privilege pairs naturally with separation of duties – structuring a sensitive process so no single person controls it end to end, so the person who approves a purchase is never also the person who can issue the payment. It also pairs with just-in-time (JIT) access, where elevated privilege is requested for a specific, time-bound window rather than sitting active and exploitable all day, expiring automatically once that window closes.

Common Misconception

“Giving admin rights to everyone is low-risk if you trust your employees.” Trust does not shrink blast radius. A trusted employee’s account can still be phished or infected by malware, and malware inherits the privileges of whoever runs it – over-provisioning turns any single compromise into an organization-wide one regardless of how trustworthy the account holder is.

Check Your Understanding

1. A system administrator stores user credentials in a database. Which of the following best identifies the primary purpose of hashing passwords before storing them?

Hashing is a one-way function that transforms a password into a fixed-length digest. Because it is computationally infeasible to reverse, even if the database is breached, the original passwords are not directly exposed. This is a preventive control that mitigates the risk of credential theft from stored data. Encryption (A) is two-way and requires a key; Base64 (D) is encoding, not a security control; compression (B) offers no security benefit.

2. Which of the following best identifies an example of multi-factor authentication (MFA)?

MFA requires credentials from two or more distinct factor categories: something you know, something you have, or something you are. A password (knowledge factor) combined with a smartphone push approval (possession factor) satisfies this requirement. Choice C uses only one factor. Choice B combines two knowledge factors (both "something you know"), which does not constitute MFA. Choice D also uses only the knowledge factor twice.

3. Which of the following authentication credentials is classified as a "something you are" factor?

Authentication factors are categorized as something you know (knowledge), something you have (possession), or something you are (inherence/biometrics). A fingerprint is a biometric attribute inherent to the user's physical person, making it a "something you are" factor. A PIN (D) is knowledge; a hardware token (B) and an SMS code (C) are possession factors, since both depend on a physical device the user holds.

Review Questions

  1. Explain, using search-space math, why a policy that mandates length and unpredictability outperforms one that mandates complexity (uppercase, digits, symbols) against both brute-force and dictionary attacks.
  2. A user enters a password, then is asked to answer a security question. Explain why this does not constitute true multi-factor authentication, and identify the minimal change that would make it qualify.
  3. An attacker sends a victim a link containing a pre-set session ID, and the victim then logs in normally. Explain why this is session fixation rather than session hijacking, and identify the single server-side control that would have prevented it regardless of what the attacker did.
  4. A marketing employee transfers to engineering and receives new access, but their old marketing-drive access is never revoked. Name this phenomenon and explain the specific organizational process that is designed to catch and correct it.
  5. Explain why RBAC and ABAC are not interchangeable, using the finance-department example from this chapter to illustrate what ABAC can express that RBAC cannot.
  6. In the Change Healthcare breach, a single compromised username and password was sufficient to reach a remote-access portal and, eventually, cascade into a nationwide outage. Explain specifically what MFA enrollment would have required of the attacker beyond the stolen password, and why the absence of that single control is described as the root cause rather than a contributing factor.
  7. Using the fixation-versus-hijacking timeline, explain why deploying only session regeneration at login, with no HttpOnly or Secure cookie flags, would still leave a system fully exposed to one of the two attack families covered in this section.
  8. A security team notices thousands of failed logins spread across many different accounts, each attempt trying only one or two common passwords, with no single account ever crossing its lockout threshold. Name this attack, explain why per-account lockout fails to stop it, and explain how it differs from credential stuffing, a related attack that also spans many accounts.
  9. Explain what storing a salted, slow hash of a password instead of the password itself protects against when a credential database is stolen. Using the 2012 LinkedIn breach, explain what it does not protect against if the salt or the slow algorithm is missing.

Answer Key

  1. Entropy is \(H = L \times \log_2(R)\): increasing length \(L\) grows the exponent that both brute-force and dictionary attacks must search. Increasing complexity only grows \(R\), the alphabet size, and human-chosen “complex” passwords draw from a far smaller effective \(R\) than the nominal 95-character keyboard, because people reuse the same handful of substitution patterns. A long random passphrase of the same or even shorter apparent length (e.g., four Diceware words at \(H \approx 51.5\) bits versus an eight-character complex password at \(H \approx 52.6\) bits) matches or beats complexity-based policy. Extending the passphrase by two more words adds over 25 bits, an increase no realistic complexity mandate on a short password can replicate. 4.2.B
  2. Both factors – the password and the security question – fall into the same category, something you know; true MFA requires evidence from two or more independent categories, not two secrets from the same category. The minimal change is replacing the security question with a factor from a different category, such as a TOTP code (something you have) or a fingerprint (something you are). 4.2.C
  3. This is session fixation because the attacker planted a known session ID before the victim ever authenticated, rather than stealing an already-authenticated token afterward. Session regeneration – issuing a brand-new random session ID at the moment of login – would have prevented it regardless of the attacker’s method. The attacker’s pre-planted ID is discarded and never becomes attached to the authenticated session. 4.2.D
  4. This phenomenon is privilege creep. A periodic access review is the organizational process designed to catch it: on a fixed schedule, every access grant a user holds must be explicitly re-approved, and any access not re-approved – including the marketing employee’s stale drive access – is revoked outright. 4.2.E
  5. RBAC assigns permissions to a role and grants them whenever a user holds that role, regardless of context. ABAC can express a conditional policy such as “allow only if department is finance and the time is business hours and the device is company-managed” instead, which requires evaluating multiple contextual attributes at request time rather than a fixed role membership. RBAC cannot encode the time-of-day or device-management conditions at all without creating a combinatorial explosion of narrower roles. 4.2.E
  6. MFA enrollment would have required the attacker to also possess a second, independent factor – a registered device, a hardware key, or a biometric – something the stolen password alone could never supply, regardless of how the password was obtained. It is described as the root cause, not a contributing factor, because every later stage of the breach (lateral movement, ransomware deployment, the nationwide outage) was contingent on that first successful login succeeding with a password alone. Had a second factor been required, the identical stolen credential would have granted the attacker nothing at all, stopping the entire chain at its first step. 4.2.C
  7. Session regeneration defeats fixation by discarding any session ID an attacker planted before login, but it does nothing to protect a token after a legitimate login has already happened. Without HttpOnly, a successful XSS injection can still read the live session cookie directly out of the page’s JavaScript context; without Secure, the cookie can still be transmitted over an unencrypted connection and sniffed. A system with regeneration alone is therefore still fully exposed to session hijacking, even though it has completely closed off fixation. 4.2.D
  8. Password spraying tries one or two common passwords against thousands of accounts, instead of exhausting many passwords against one account. It deliberately stays under each account’s lockout threshold by never repeating a guess on the same one. Per-account lockout only counts failures on a single account, so a scheme that never trips that count goes unnoticed; the defense instead has to watch the account population as a whole, such as rate-limiting total failed logins organization-wide. Credential stuffing differs in what it guesses with: it replays real username/password pairs leaked from an unrelated breach, betting on password reuse, rather than guessing common passwords blind – the matching defense is screening logins against known-breached credential pairs, not lockout. 4.2.B
  9. Storing a salted, slow hash means a stolen database never hands an attacker plaintext passwords directly; the attacker only gets something they still have to guess and check, one candidate at a time, against each hash. It does not protect against the guessing itself. LinkedIn’s 2012 breach used unsalted SHA-1 with no slow key-stretching step, so precomputed rainbow tables cracked the unsalted hashes instantly. A GPU tested billions of guesses per second against the fast algorithm; more than 60% of the exposed hashes fell within a single day. Salting alone would have defeated the rainbow tables; a slow algorithm alone would have made each guess expensive; LinkedIn had neither, which is why both properties have to hold at once. 4.2.A

Sources

  1. [1] National Institute of Standards and Technology, Special Publication 800-63B: Digital Identity Guidelines – Authentication and Lifecycle Management, June 2017.
  2. [2] Brian Krebs, “As Scope of 2012 Breach Expands, LinkedIn to Again Reset Passwords for Some Users,” KrebsOnSecurity, May 18, 2016 (117 million accounts, unsalted, unstretched SHA-1 hashes); cracking-rate figure per Computerworld, “Hackers Crack More Than 60% of Breached LinkedIn Passwords,” June 2012, citing Sophos researcher Chester Wisniewski.
  3. [3] Bill Toulas, “Change Healthcare Hacked Using Stolen Citrix Account With No MFA,” BleepingComputer, April 30, 2024; Andrew Witty (CEO, UnitedHealth Group), written testimony before the U.S. House Energy and Commerce Committee, May 1, 2024; cost figure per UnitedHealth Group’s Q2 2024 earnings disclosure, July 16, 2024, updated in subsequent quarterly filings.

4.3 Endpoint Hardening Controls

An ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure.

Benjamin Franklin (1736)

Motivation

Chapter 4.2 controlled who is allowed to authenticate. This chapter is about the device itself, independent of who is logging into it. How does it arrive from the factory? What actively watches it while it runs? What happens after a vendor discovers a flaw in it? The three sections build a timeline: harden the device before it is ever deployed, defend it continuously while it operates, and close the specific flaws vendors find in it over its whole operational life.

AP Course Alignment

Unit 4: Securing Devices · Topic 4.3 – Protecting Devices

  • 4.3.A Mitigate Risk Apply the four categories of device hardening – changing default credentials, disabling unneeded services and ports, enabling protective features the vendor ships off by default, and restricting accounts and privileges – and explain how configuration-management tooling detects and corrects the configuration drift that erodes a hardening baseline over time.
  • 4.3.B Mitigate Risk Account for how anti-malware software actually reaches a verdict – by identity, by structure, or by observed behavior – and state what each of those three procedures can and cannot see, including why no update schedule allows a signature to match code that has never been analyzed.
  • 4.3.C Mitigate Risk Explain why applying operating-system and application updates reduces risk in a specific rather than general way: each fix removes the particular defect an exploit depends on, and the interval between a public exploit and a deployed fix is the window in which most compromise happens.
