  • 4.3.D Mitigate Risk Set up a firewall on the device itself – default action, per-network profile, ordered or precedence-based rules restricted by port, source, and program, and logging – and be able to say which traffic this catches that a firewall at the network boundary never sees.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

Device Hardening

Every device ships from the factory configured for maximum compatibility and ease of setup, not security. Identical default administrator passwords ship across thousands of units. Unneeded services run because a manufacturer assumed someone might want them. Open remote-management ports are left convenient for a technician.

Definition: Device hardening

The systematic process of closing unnecessary doors before a device is deployed, and periodically re-closing them as configurations drift. The goal is to reduce the device’s attack surface – the total set of ways an attacker could interact with it to cause harm.

The 2016 Mirai botnet from Chapter 4.1 is the canonical illustration of what an unhardened device costs at scale: it was built almost entirely from IoT devices still running factory credentials nobody had ever changed.[1] Hardening frameworks like CIS Benchmarks and NIST SP 800-70 decompose the practice into four categories. Change defaults: every default password – the OS account, the BIOS/UEFI firmware interface, a router or camera’s built-in web panel – must become a unique, strong credential. Default credentials are publicly documented, so there is no secrecy to rely on. Remove or disable what is not needed: every service, protocol, and open port not actively required by the device’s function is an unnecessary risk. The governing question for each one is simply whether the device’s legitimate business function requires it. Enable protective features that ship off by default because they add friction: host-based firewalls, full-disk encryption, automatic screen lock, Secure Boot to block unsigned code at startup. Lock down accounts and privileges: disable built-in guest accounts, rename or disable the default administrator/root account, and require explicit privilege elevation (sudo, UAC) for administrative tasks rather than running everything as root.

A hardened device does not stay hardened. Administrators install new software, enable a port “temporarily” for a project, or reset a setting during troubleshooting, and each change is a potential re-opening of a closed door – a phenomenon called configuration drift. Professional security programs address this with configuration-management tools (Ansible, Puppet, Group Policy) that continuously compare a device’s live state against its known-good baseline and alert on, or automatically correct, any deviation.

Common Misconception

“Changing the password is enough.” Changing the default password is the single most important first step, but a genuinely hardened device also disables unneeded services, enables protective features, and restricts account privileges – password change alone leaves the other three categories untouched.

Antivirus and Endpoint Protection

Even on a fully hardened device, a user still has to open files, visit websites, and plug in external media – and every one of those interactions is an opportunity for malware to run.

Definition: Signature-based detection

A malware-detection method that matches files or memory against a database of unique byte sequences extracted from known malware. Fast and accurate for cataloged threats, but blind by construction to anything not yet in the database.

Signature detection’s blind spot is exactly what polymorphic malware exploits: code that changes its shape or re-encrypts itself on every compilation, altering its signature while its malicious function stays identical. The same threat evades a signature check indefinitely without ever changing what it actually does. Heuristic analysis answers this by scanning a program’s code for patterns commonly associated with malware – suspicious API calls, instructions that modify critical system files – rather than matching an exact fingerprint. Behavioral analysis goes further still, watching a program as it runs. A seemingly harmless utility that suddenly injects code into another process, opens a raw network socket, and begins encrypting user files in rapid succession is flagged and terminated as characteristic of ransomware – entirely independent of whether its file signature matches anything on record. The tradeoff is direct: behavioral analysis catches genuinely novel threats that signatures cannot, but it is computationally expensive and carries a higher false-positive rate, since legitimate administrative software can perform some of the same actions a real attack would.

Definition: Host-based firewall

Security software running directly on a single device – distinct from the network firewall at an organization’s perimeter – that filters inbound and outbound traffic against local rules.

A host-based firewall closes two specific gaps a perimeter firewall cannot. First, it blocks an already compromised machine on the same local network from reaching services on this device at all. Second, it blocks malware that does manage to run on this device from ever reaching its command-and-control (C2) server outbound. That second gap renders an otherwise-successful infection functionally useless, because the malware can never receive instructions or exfiltrate anything.

Configuring a host-based firewall on Linux and Windows

On a Linux endpoint, ufw (Uncomplicated Firewall) is a common front end for the kernel’s own netfilter rules. A minimal hardening baseline denies everything inbound by default and explicitly allows only the ports the device’s function actually requires:

On Windows, the equivalent baseline is set through the NetSecurity PowerShell module rather than a GUI, which is what makes it scriptable across a fleet via configuration management:

Both examples encode the same underlying policy: a default-deny inbound posture with narrow, named exceptions. That is precisely the traffic a perimeter firewall cannot enforce once two devices are already talking on the same local segment.

Managing antivirus and firewall configuration across thousands of individual devices by hand is impractical. That is why enterprises deploy an Endpoint Protection Platform (EPP): a single agent per device combining antivirus, host firewall, intrusion prevention, and data-loss prevention, all reporting to one central console. That console lets administrators monitor fleet-wide status, push updates uniformly, and remotely isolate a compromised device with a single click.

Common Misconception

“Having an antivirus means I don’t need a firewall.” Antivirus blocks malicious files and code execution; a firewall regulates network traffic. They defend against different vectors entirely, and neither one substitutes for the other.

Patching and Regular Updates

Antivirus and firewalls defend a device from the outside, watching for known-bad files and traffic. Many of history’s most damaging breaches needed neither a tricked user nor a signature evasion at all – they exploited a known, published vulnerability that the victim simply had not yet fixed.

Definition: CVE (Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures)

A public, unique identifier assigned to a disclosed software vulnerability, typically accompanied by a CVSS severity score from 0 to 10 describing how easy and damaging exploitation would be.

A vulnerability’s lifecycle runs through discovery, responsible disclosure to the vendor with time to fix it, and patch release. Then comes weaponization – attackers reverse-engineering the patch itself, sometimes within hours, since a patch’s code diff directly reveals exactly what was broken and how. The moment a patch ships, the vulnerability effectively becomes public knowledge regardless of whether an exploit yet exists. A race begins between defenders applying the fix and attackers building a working exploit from the disclosure.

Zero-day versus n-day

A zero-day vulnerability is being actively exploited before any patch exists at all. There are zero days of protection available even for a fully up-to-date system, and only other layered defenses (behavioral detection, network segmentation) can help. An n-day vulnerability is one for which a patch has existed for some number of days, but the target system simply has not applied it yet. The overwhelming majority of real-world breaches exploit n-day vulnerabilities, not zero-days. Reusing a known exploit against an unpatched target is far cheaper for an attacker than discovering something genuinely new, and most organizations lag behind on patching long enough to make that bet reliably pay off.

CVE-2017-0144 and the WannaCry exploit window

CVE-2017-0144, an SMBv1 remote-code-execution flaw in Windows (CVSS v3 base score 8.8), illustrates the patch-to-weaponization race precisely. Microsoft shipped the fix as bulletin MS17-010 on March 14, 2017.[2] The exploit built around that flaw, code-named EternalBlue, was leaked publicly by the Shadow Brokers group on April 14, 2017 – one month after the patch. Just under two months after the original patch, on May 12, 2017, the WannaCry ransomware worm combined EternalBlue with a self-propagation mechanism to infect more than 200,000 machines across 150 countries within days.[3] Every one of those machines had gone unpatched despite the fix already sitting on Microsoft’s servers for that entire window. WannaCry is not a zero-day case study; every infected machine was vulnerable to a flaw with a known fix, which is exactly what makes it the canonical n-day breach rather than a genuinely novel attack.

Patching is not free, which is the actual reason unpatched year-old vulnerabilities remain common despite patching’s obvious value. A patch can break a business-critical application that depends on old behavior. Some patches require a reboot that must be scheduled around business needs. A large enterprise may have tens of thousands of endpoints, each needing separate validation. Some critical systems run software the vendor no longer supports at all (end-of-life), meaning no patch will ever exist and only compensating controls like network isolation remain available.

Mature organizations run a formal patch management process rather than patching ad hoc. First, inventory what is running, since a patch cannot be applied to an unknown asset. Next, prioritize new patches by CVSS severity, whether exploitation is already occurring in the wild, and whether the affected service is internet-facing. Test in a non-production environment to catch compatibility breaks before they hit production. Deploy in waves on a defined patch cadence, reserving emergency out-of-cycle deployment for critical, actively exploited flaws. Finally, verify the patch was actually applied fleet-wide – partial or failed rollouts are common, and they leave silent, undetected gaps behind.

Common Misconception

“Zero-days are the main way organizations get breached.” Most real-world breaches exploit n-day vulnerabilities – known flaws with an available patch that simply was not applied in time. Zero-days are comparatively rare and expensive for an attacker to obtain or develop.

Check Your Understanding

1. A company wants to reduce device security risk across all departments. Which of the following is best classified as a managerial control for device security?

Managerial (administrative) controls are governance and policy measures, things like acceptable use policies, security plans, and risk assessments. Configuring antivirus (A), enforcing screen-lock timeouts (C), and installing firewalls (D) are all technical controls applied directly to devices.

2. Which statement best describes how anti-malware software helps protect a device?

Anti-malware software scans for, detects, and removes or quarantines malicious programs, before execution (on-access scanning) or after (on-demand scanning). It operates at the endpoint level on individual files and processes.

3. What is the primary security reason an organization should apply operating system updates promptly?

OS updates frequently include security patches that close known vulnerabilities. Once a CVE is published, adversaries can exploit unpatched systems. Promptly applying updates reduces the window of exposure to those known vulnerabilities.

Review Questions

  1. A newly purchased IoT camera has its default administrator password unchanged and Telnet enabled by default. Using the four-category hardening checklist from this chapter, identify which two categories this failure falls under and explain why changing only the password would leave the device still exposed.
  2. Explain why polymorphic malware defeats signature-based detection while leaving its actual malicious behavior completely unchanged, and explain which detection method in this chapter is specifically designed to catch it anyway.
  3. Malware successfully executes on a hardened, antivirus-protected laptop but never manages to contact its command-and-control server. Explain which specific control is responsible for that outcome and why it renders the infection functionally useless despite having already run.
  4. A CVE is disclosed alongside its patch. Explain why attackers can sometimes weaponize the vulnerability within hours of the patch’s release, even though the patch was meant to fix the problem, not enable new attacks.
  5. Explain why an organization might rationally choose to delay applying a released patch for several days rather than deploying it immediately, and explain how a formal patch-management process reduces the risk of that delay without eliminating it entirely.
  6. Using the vulnerability-lifecycle diagram, explain why WannaCry is placed at the “n-day exploit” stage rather than the “weaponization” stage, even though EternalBlue itself was built by reverse-engineering a patch.
  7. A CISO argues that since most breaches are n-day rather than zero-day, patching quickly matters more than funding zero-day threat research. Using the vulnerability-lifecycle diagram and the patch-urgency logic from this chapter, explain what evidence in this chapter supports that argument, and identify one category of system (from the patching-is-not-free discussion) where speed alone cannot close the gap.

Answer Key

  1. The unchanged default password falls under change defaults, and the enabled Telnet service falls under remove or disable what is not needed. Changing only the password would leave Telnet – an insecure, plaintext remote-management protocol – still reachable, giving an attacker a second independent path onto the device that the password change never touched. 4.3.A
  2. Polymorphic malware re-encrypts or reshapes its own code on every compilation, changing its byte-level signature while its underlying malicious function stays identical, so a signature database built on the old fingerprint never matches the new one. Behavioral analysis is designed to catch it anyway, since it watches what a program actually does at runtime – injecting code, opening raw sockets, mass-encrypting files – rather than matching against a static fingerprint. 4.3.B
  3. A host-based firewall is responsible: it blocks outbound traffic from the infected device to any command-and-control server the malware attempts to reach. This renders the infection functionally useless despite having already executed, because the malware can neither receive further instructions nor exfiltrate any data it collected. 4.3.D
  4. A released patch’s binary diff directly reveals which code paths changed. That lets an attacker reverse-engineer exactly what was broken and reconstruct a working exploit from the comparison alone – as CVE-2017-0144 showed, where EternalBlue was built from the same vulnerability class MS17-010 had already patched. The patch fixes the flaw for defenders who apply it, but its public release simultaneously hands attackers a precise roadmap to the flaw for anyone who has not. 4.3.C
  5. A patch can break a business-critical application depending on old behavior, may require a disruptive reboot, and at enterprise scale needs individual validation across tens of thousands of endpoints. Immediate blanket deployment carries its own operational risk. A formal patch management process reduces, without eliminating, that delay’s risk: it prioritizes the patch using CVSS severity and active-exploitation status, tests it in a non-production environment first, and deploys it on a defined cadence, with emergency out-of-cycle deployment reserved for critical, actively exploited flaws. 4.3.C
  6. Weaponization is the act of reverse-engineering a working exploit from the patch, which happened quickly for EternalBlue. But WannaCry itself did not strike until May 12, 2017, nearly two months after the March 14 patch, and every machine it infected was one that had not yet applied a fix that had been available the entire time. WannaCry is the exploitation of that already-weaponized flaw against unpatched targets, which is precisely the definition of the n-day stage: the exploit existed and worked, but the window it succeeded in was created by delayed patching, not by any remaining novelty in the vulnerability itself. 4.3.C
  7. The chapter’s own claim that most real-world breaches exploit n-day rather than zero-day vulnerabilities supports the CISO’s argument, as does the vulnerability-lifecycle diagram: it shows the n-day window opening the moment a patch ships and staying open until every affected system is patched. Closing that window faster removes far more real-world risk than defending against the comparatively rare zero-day case. The category where speed alone cannot close the gap is end-of-life systems. A vendor that no longer supports the software will never release a patch to deploy quickly in the first place, so no patch-management process, however fast, can close that specific n-day window – only compensating controls like network isolation remain available. 4.3.C

Sources

  1. [1] M. Antonakakis, T. April, M. Bailey, et al., “Understanding the Mirai Botnet,” 26th USENIX Security Symposium, Vancouver, August 16–18, 2017, pp. 1093–1110 – the peer-reviewed analysis documenting Mirai’s compromise of IoT devices through roughly 60 hardcoded factory-default Telnet username/password pairs.
  2. [2] Microsoft Corporation, Security Bulletin MS17-010, “Security Update for Microsoft Windows SMB Server,” released March 14, 2017, addressing CVE-2017-0144 (CVSS v3 base score 8.8).
  3. [3] Cloudflare, “What Was the WannaCry Ransomware Attack?,” Cloudflare Learning Center – reporting the Shadow Brokers’ April 14, 2017 public leak of the EternalBlue exploit and WannaCry’s spread to more than 200,000 computers across over 150 countries beginning May 12, 2017.

4.4 Endpoint Threat Auditing

It is a capital mistake to theorize before one has all the evidence.

Arthur Conan Doyle, A Study in Scarlet (1887)

Motivation

Chapter 4.3 hardened the device, watched it continuously, and patched its known flaws. This closing chapter of Unit 4 asks the question those controls cannot fully answer on their own: given everything that happened on a device, was it actually compromised? Three complementary disciplines answer this together – live behavioral monitoring on the device itself, correlating historical log entries across time, and combining scattered forensic clues into one confident verdict. None of the three is sufficient alone; each catches what the others miss.

AP Course Alignment

Unit 4: Securing Devices · Topic 4.4 – Detecting Attacks on Devices

  • 4.4.A Detect Attacks Account for how an attack on a device is noticed at all: what the operating system and its security tooling record, why that record has to leave the machine before an attacker can reach it, and what makes a sequence of events evidence when no single one of them is.
  • 4.4.B Detect Attacks Choose the detective controls that suit a given device and a given threat – audit policy and the events it turns on, off-host forwarding, correlation logic, tamper-evident retention – and be able to say which attack each one would and would not catch.
  • 4.4.C Detect Attacks Judge a detection method by what it does in practice: the coverage it provides, the volume it generates, whether the people receiving its output can act on it, and the failure it will still miss – recognizing that a rule nobody can triage lowers detection capability rather than raising it.
  • 4.4.D Detect Attacks Work through real authentication logs and identify the traces of a credential attack, separating exhaustive guessing against one account from low-and-slow guessing across many, and stating which distinctions the log can support and which it cannot.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

Endpoint Detection and Response

A firewall guards the network boundary, but once malicious code is already running inside a machine, a boundary control cannot see it at all.

Definition: Endpoint Detection and Response (EDR)

Security software that runs as an agent on a device, continuously collecting telemetry – process, file system, registry, network, and user-account events – and using both signature matching and behavioral modeling to detect and respond to threats on the endpoint itself.

The telemetry an EDR agent records is dense. It includes process events (every program starting, stopping, or spawning a child), file system events (creation, modification, deletion), and registry changes on Windows (a common mechanism for persistence, letting malware restart after a reboot). It also includes network connections (which process opened a socket, to where, and how much data moved) and user-account events (logins, privilege escalation). Signature matching compares this stream against known-bad indicators, fast and precise for cataloged threats but blind to anything novel, exactly as in Chapter 4.3. Behavioral detection instead builds a model of what normal looks like on a specific host and flags deviations from it. That catches not just novel malware but living-off-the-land techniques, where an attacker misuses entirely legitimate tools like PowerShell or WMI to carry out malicious actions. No file-based signature would ever flag those actions, because the tool itself is not malware.

A living-off-the-land chain

An employee opens an email attachment whose macro uses PowerShell – a legitimate Windows tool – to download a second-stage payload. Traditional antivirus misses this entirely, since PowerShell itself is not malicious. An EDR agent instead records the full chain: an unusual parent-child relationship (the Office application spawned PowerShell) and an unusual outbound network connection from a process that normally never touches the network. It also records a new executable written to a temp directory. No single event in that chain is damning on its own, but the behavioral model scores the combination as high-risk and can isolate the laptop automatically before the payload ever reaches another system.

Detection is only the first step. EDR is distinguished by its response capability once confidence is high enough: it can alert a SOC with full telemetry context rather than one isolated event; isolate the endpoint from the network to stop lateral movement while leaving an analyst able to investigate remotely; kill the malicious process outright; quarantine a file; or preserve forensic evidence with a memory or disk snapshot.

Common Misconception

“EDR is just better antivirus.” Antivirus primarily checks files against known signatures at rest. EDR monitors continuous runtime behavior across process, file, network, and registry streams simultaneously, and can respond by isolating, killing, or quarantining – not merely scanning and reporting.

Log Analysis

EDR watches live behavior on one device; logs extend that visibility across time and across every system that generates one – operating systems, web servers, firewalls, authentication systems.

Definition: Correlation

Linking multiple log entries together – by the same user, the same source IP, or the same time window – to reveal a pattern that no single entry shows on its own.

A single failed login line is entirely ordinary; anyone mistypes a password occasionally. Five failed attempts for the same username within a ten-second window, all from the same source IP, is a different fact entirely. No single line in that sequence looks alarming in isolation, but the pattern itself is a textbook brute-force signature: rapid, clustered failures targeting one account. Analysts formalize this into a detection rule: flag any account with five or more failures within any sixty-second window. A SIEM platform then runs rules like this automatically across every log source in an organization at once.

None of this works if the attacker can simply delete the evidence. An attacker with local administrator access can clear the Windows Event Log or edit auth.log directly, erasing exactly the entries that would reveal the intrusion. This is why detection programs forward logs off the host, ideally in near-real-time, to a centralized collector or SIEM. Once a copy of an event leaves the machine that generated it, an attacker who compromises that machine afterward can no longer reach it. Centralized storage adds a second layer with tamper-evident retention – write-once storage or cryptographically chained log entries, where altering or deleting a past entry breaks a verifiable chain and is immediately detectable. Together, off-host forwarding and tamper-evident retention answer a question local logging alone cannot: not just what happened, but whether the record of what happened can still be trusted.

Reading real authentication events

On Windows, a failed logon is recorded as Event ID 4625, and its Status and Sub Status fields distinguish why it failed. Sub status 0xC000006A means the account name was correct but the password was wrong (a genuine guess). Sub status 0xC0000064 means the account name itself does not exist, which is useful for spotting an attacker enumerating usernames. Event ID 4624 is the corresponding successful logon, recorded on the machine the session was created on. Event ID 4740 fires when an account is locked out after too many failures. On a domain using Kerberos, Event ID 4771 records a failed Kerberos pre-authentication instead – a Kerberos-specific failure signal distinct from 4625. A detection rule built only around 4625 would silently miss it entirely.[1]

4625 (repeated) \(\rightarrow\) 4740 (lockout) \(\rightarrow\) 4624 (success, new IP)

is the signature of a single account under sustained attack. On Linux, the equivalent evidence lives in /var/log/auth.log:

No single line above is alarming by itself, but the same correlation principle applies. Repeated failures for one account from one source, followed by success, at 3 a.m., form the pattern – not the final "Accepted" line read in isolation.

Threshold tuning here is the same tension seen throughout this course. A threshold set too low – flagging after two failures – generates constant false alarms from users who simply mistyped a password, training analysts into alert fatigue. A threshold set too high, on the other hand, lets a real, deliberately slow brute-force attempt pass through undetected. Logs matter beyond real-time detection as well: they are frequently the only record of what an attacker did once that attacker has already left the system. That makes them essential for forensics – reconstructing an incident after the fact rather than catching it as it happens.

Distinguishing a brute-force attack from a password spray

The brute-force detector above watches one account for many failures. A password spray inverts that pattern: an attacker tries one or two common passwords against many different accounts from the same source. The attacker deliberately stays under any single account’s lockout threshold, so the per-account brute-force rule never fires at all. Detecting a spray requires correlating by source IP across the whole account population instead of by username:

This function was run against a synthetic 20-account, one-source-IP event log during preparation of this chapter. It correctly returned the spraying IP once that IP touched eight distinct accounts within the five-minute window, while leaving a single legitimate user’s occasional mistyped password unflagged. The distinction matters operationally: a rule tuned only to catch many failures on one account is structurally blind to a spray, since no individual account in a spray ever crosses that account’s own threshold.

Common Misconception

“A single failed login is proof of an attack.” One failed login is ordinary human error. Attacks are recognized through correlated patterns – multiple entries linked by user, IP, and a tight time window – never from one log line in isolation.

Indicators of Compromise

EDR and log analysis both ultimately produce the same kind of output: individual clues that something may be wrong.

Definition: Indicator of compromise (IOC)

A piece of forensic evidence – an artifact left behind on a system or network – that suggests a device has been breached.

IOCs come from several layers. File-based IOCs include a file matching a known-malicious hash or an unexpected executable in a startup directory. Network-based IOCs include a connection to a known command-and-control IP or DNS lookups for algorithmically generated domain names, a technique malware uses to find its C2 server without ever hardcoding one address. Host-based IOCs include a new administrator account nobody remembers creating, a persistence mechanism like an unowned scheduled task, or antivirus protection silently disabled. Behavioral IOCs are patterns rather than single artifacts – a login at 3 a.m. from an account that has never logged in outside business hours.

Why one IOC is rarely a verdict

A new administrator account appears on a server. Alone, this could easily be an IT technician’s legitimate maintenance action, or it could be an attacker establishing persistence – the IOC alone cannot distinguish the two. If that same account appears alongside a disabled antivirus service and a connection to a known-malicious IP, the combination becomes far more convincing. Three independent artifacts pointing toward the same conclusion form dramatically stronger evidence than any one of them alone. That is the identical correlation principle from log analysis, applied here to compromise evidence broadly rather than login timestamps specifically.

Because different IOC types carry very different severity, mature detection systems assign each a weight rather than simply counting how many are present. A confirmed known-malicious file hash is strong evidence and receives a high weight, while a handful of failed logins is comparatively weak evidence and receives a low one. Summing every observed IOC’s weight produces a compromise score, compared against thresholds to reach a verdict: clean below a low threshold, suspicious in a middle band warranting investigation, and compromised – warranting immediate isolation – above a high one. That is the same weighted-scoring logic CVSS applies to vulnerabilities in Chapter 4.3.

An indicator of attack (IOA) is a related but distinct concept. It focuses on an attacker’s intent and technique while it is happening – a process attempting to enumerate every file with a specific extension, which is exactly what ransomware does moments before encrypting them. An IOC, by contrast, is typically evidence left after a step has already occurred, such as a file with a .locked extension that already exists. IOAs aim to catch an attack in progress; IOCs confirm that something already happened. Real detection programs use both, which is the point of studying EDR, log correlation, and IOC scoring together rather than as three unrelated tools.

Common Misconception

“Any single suspicious artifact proves a device is compromised.” One IOC alone is usually ambiguous and often has an entirely innocent explanation. Confident verdicts come from combining multiple independent IOCs, exactly as correlating multiple log entries does in the previous section.

Check Your Understanding

1. A security analyst wants to monitor activities on a single workstation for signs of attack originating on or targeting that machine. Which type of tool is specifically designed to detect attacks by monitoring events on the host device itself?

A HIDS monitors events on the host device, file changes, process activity, login events, to detect attacks occurring on or against that specific machine. A NIDS monitors network traffic passing through a segment; a WAF filters web-layer HTTP requests; a packet analyzer captures and decodes raw network frames. None of those three operate at the individual host level for general endpoint event detection.

2. In the context of device security, which term describes observable evidence found on or produced by a host that suggests a security incident may have occurred or be in progress?

An Indicator of Compromise (IoC) is observable evidence, such as unusual log entries, unexpected processes, unfamiliar file hashes, or anomalous network connections, that indicates a device may have been compromised or is under active attack. Vulnerabilities are weaknesses that could be exploited; threat vectors are the pathways through which an attack reaches a system; attack surface is the aggregate of all exploitable entry points on a system.

3. An administrator reviews an authentication log and finds 350 failed login attempts targeting the "admin" account from the same IP address, all occurring within a two-minute window. Which attack does this pattern most clearly indicate?

A high volume of failed login attempts against a single account from a single source in rapid succession is the textbook signature of a brute-force attack, where an attacker systematically submits password guesses. SQL injection exploits database query inputs; XSS injects malicious scripts into web pages; MITM attacks intercept traffic between two parties. None of those three produce repeated authentication failures in a log.

Review Questions

  1. An email macro launches PowerShell, which then opens an outbound network connection. Explain why traditional signature-based antivirus is likely to miss this chain entirely, and explain which EDR detection method catches it instead and why.
  2. An authentication log shows five failed logins for one account within eight seconds, all from the same IP, followed by a successful login from that same IP. Explain why reading the final successful login in isolation is misleading, and describe the correlation that reveals the true pattern.
  3. A detection rule is tuned to flag any account with two or more failed logins in a day. Explain the specific operational problem this creates and name the phenomenon it produces among analysts.
  4. An organization keeps its authentication logs only on each local device, with no forwarding to a central collector. Explain the specific evidentiary weakness this creates once an attacker gains administrative access to a device, and explain what tamper-evident retention adds even after logs are forwarded somewhere else.
  5. A server shows a new administrator account and a disabled antivirus service, but no known- malicious network connection. Using the weighted-scoring approach from this chapter, explain why this combination might still cross a "suspicious" threshold even without every category of IOC present.
  6. Explain the difference between an indicator of compromise and an indicator of attack, and explain why a mature detection program needs both rather than relying on just one.
  7. A security team notices that a source IP triggers no per-account brute-force alert despite having failed logins against twenty different accounts over a few minutes. Explain why the brute-force detector from this chapter misses this entirely, and describe the specific correlation dimension a password-spray detector uses instead.
  8. Using the EDR telemetry diagram, explain why an attacker using only living-off-the-land techniques (legitimate tools like PowerShell, no custom malware file at all) can still be caught, even though every individual telemetry stream – the process list, the file system, the registry – shows nothing that would trigger a signature match on its own.

Answer Key

  1. Traditional signature-based antivirus checks files against known-malicious hashes at rest; PowerShell itself is a legitimate signed Windows binary with no malicious signature to match, so it never triggers a file-based check. EDR’s behavioral detection instead catches the chain by scoring the unusual combination of events – an Office application spawning PowerShell, an unexpected outbound network connection, a new file written to a temp directory. None of those events is damning alone, but together they score as high-risk living-off-the-land activity. 4.4.A
  2. Reading only the final successful login is misleading because, in isolation, one successful login line looks entirely ordinary. The correlation that reveals the true pattern links the five failed attempts to the same username, the same source IP, and a tight eight-second window. Together those link to Event ID 4625 with sub status 0xC000006A repeated in rapid succession, followed by the eventual 4624 success – a textbook brute-force signature. It only becomes visible once the entries are linked rather than read one at a time. 4.4.D
  3. A threshold of two failures in a day is set far too low: ordinary users who simply mistype a password will routinely cross it, generating constant false alarms. The phenomenon this produces among analysts is alert fatigue, where a flood of low-value alerts trains analysts to deprioritize or ignore the detection rule entirely, which paradoxically lowers real detection capability. 4.4.C
  4. Using the weighted-scoring approach, a new unapproved admin account (weight 5) plus a disabled antivirus service (weight 6) sums to 11. That exceeds the suspicious threshold of 6, even though no network-based IOC (such as a C2 connection) is present. The scoring model does not require every IOC category to be represented. It only requires the sum of observed weights to cross a threshold – two independent, moderately weighted artifacts together can be as convincing as one heavily weighted one. 4.4.C
  5. Local-only logs are only as trustworthy as the device that generates them: once an attacker has administrative access, they can edit or delete the very Event ID or auth.log entries that would reveal the intrusion, destroying the evidence before anyone reads it. Forwarding a copy of each event off the host, ideally in near-real-time, removes it from the attacker’s reach the moment it is written – the attacker would have to compromise the collector too, a categorically harder task. Tamper-evident retention closes the remaining gap: even at the centralized store, write-once storage or cryptographically chained entries mean that any later edit or deletion breaks a verifiable chain and is caught, rather than trusted silently. 4.4.B
  6. An indicator of compromise (IOC) is evidence that a step has already occurred, such as a file with a .locked extension. An indicator of attack (IOA) focuses on an attacker’s intent and technique while it is happening, such as a process enumerating every file with a specific extension moments before ransomware would encrypt them. A mature program needs both because IOAs aim to interrupt an attack in progress while it can still be stopped, while IOCs confirm what already happened and support the forensic reconstruction that follows. 4.4.B
  7. The brute-force detector correlates failures by username, counting how many times one account was attacked. A password spray deliberately stays under any single account’s threshold instead, trying only one or two passwords per account before moving to the next, so no individual username ever accumulates enough failures to fire the rule. A password-spray detector instead correlates by source IP across the entire account population, counting how many distinct usernames one source touched within a time window. The fan-out across accounts is the signal, not the failure count against any one of them. 4.4.D
  8. Living-off-the-land techniques use tools that are individually legitimate. No single telemetry stream – the process list showing PowerShell running, the file system showing a temp-directory write, the network stream showing an outbound connection – contains anything a signature check would flag, since none of those artifacts is malicious in isolation. The behavioral model in the diagram does not evaluate any one stream alone. It scores the combination across all of them at once – an unusual parent-child spawn relationship, together with unexpected network activity, together with a new file. That combined, cross-stream pattern is exactly what a purely per-stream signature check has no way to see. 4.4.B

Sources

  1. [1] Microsoft Corporation, “4625(F) An account failed to log on” and “4771(F) Kerberos pre-authentication failed,” Windows Security Auditing Events reference, Microsoft Learn – documenting the failure sub status codes 0xC000006A (wrong password) and 0xC0000064 (account does not exist), and confirming Event ID 4771 is logged only on domain controllers.

Unit 5: Securing Applications and Data

The last three units moved from a person, to a building, to a network, to a device. Unit 5 makes the final move: into the code running on that device and the data it handles. Chapter 5.1 opens with how vulnerabilities get written into applications in the first place – injection flaws, buffer overflows, unvalidated input – the software-level equivalent of the unpatched configurations from Chapter 4.1. Chapter 5.2 then applies the access-control models first introduced in Chapter 2.3 – DAC, MAC, and RBAC – to software permissions and explains how a compromised low-privilege process becomes a full breach through lateral movement when those controls are missing or misconfigured.

Chapters 5.3 and 5.4 cover the two branches of cryptography that make the rest of this unit’s defenses possible: symmetric hashing and key storage on one side, asymmetric (public-key) cryptography on the other. These chapters explain not just how the math works but why an application reaches for one over the other – hashing a password versus encrypting a message versus verifying a certificate are three different problems with three different correct tools. Chapter 5.5 then applies all of it to the concrete case of a web application, covering secure design practices like input validation, session management, and HTTPS that tie directly back to the login-page trust problem opened in Chapter 1.2.

Chapter 5.6 ends both the unit and the book with application threat auditing – the software-layer version of the detection chapters that closed every previous unit. It is a deliberate bookend: the course opened by asking how a single careless click compromises a person, and it closes by asking how an organization audits its software to catch the compromise that followed.

5.1 Application Code Vulnerabilities

When you have eliminated the impossible, whatever remains, however improbable, must be the truth.

Arthur Conan Doyle, The Sign of Four (1890)

Motivation

The final unit moves to the innermost layer of the whole course: the application code itself, and the data it stores. Every earlier unit assumed a working application underneath it – a login page, a database, an API. This chapter is about what happens when that application trusts input it should not have trusted. Three examples recur throughout: a SQL query built from unescaped user text, a page that renders a malicious script as if the developer wrote it, and an API that forgets to check whether a caller owns the object they are asking for. It closes with the aggregate consequence of all three: the data breach, and how to size up how bad one actually is.

AP Course Alignment

Unit 5: Securing Applications and Data · Topic 5.1 – Application and Data Vulnerabilities and Attacks

  • 5.1.A Analyze Risk Account for the ways an adversary turns a weakness in a program or a stored file into a concrete outcome for the organization that owns it – records read that should not have been readable, records altered or erased, or a service that stops working because the data it depends on was destroyed.
  • 5.1.B Analyze Risk Explain the mechanism by which a particular attack reaches a particular defect: which input the attacker controls, which trust boundary that input crosses unchecked, and what the receiving interpreter or check – a SQL parser, a browser’s HTML/JavaScript engine, or an API’s authorization logic – does with it once it arrives.
  • 5.1.C Analyze Risk Judge and record the risk a given application or data weakness poses, so that the finding can be ranked against other findings and handed to whoever will decide whether to fix it, defer it, or accept it.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

SQL Injection

Web applications almost universally sit on top of a relational database, and building a query typically means splicing user input directly into it.

Definition: SQL injection (SQLi)

An attack in which a user types SQL syntax instead of ordinary data, and the application pastes it unmodified into a query, causing the database to execute the attacker’s SQL as if it were the application’s own code.

The mechanism is a simple failure to separate code from data. A query built by string concatenation –

lets an attacker type admin'-- as the username. The resulting query becomes SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = 'admin'--' AND password = 'anything'; – and because -- is SQL’s comment character, everything after it is discarded, including the password check. The attacker is logged in as admin having supplied no password at all.

Attackers have catalogued recurring payload families. Authentication bypass uses OR logic – typing ' OR '1'='1 as a password makes the WHERE clause always true, returning the first user in the table, often an administrator. Comment truncation is the admin'-- pattern above. UNION-based extraction appends the attacker’s own SELECT to the original query – UNION SELECT username, password FROM users dumps credentials alongside whatever the page normally returns. Stacked queries attempt a second statement entirely, as in '; DROP TABLE users--. Time-based blind injection is used when the application shows no visible output at all: an attacker uses SLEEP(5) and infers data one bit at a time purely from whether the response is slow.

Prevention fixes the root cause rather than filtering symptoms: a parameterized query (prepared statement) passes the SQL template and the data values to the database driver separately, so the driver handles quoting and escaping and user data can never be parsed as SQL syntax, no matter what characters it contains. A Web Application Firewall can block known payloads in transit, but is a supplementary layer, not a substitute for fixing the query itself.

Common Misconception

“Encoding the output prevents injection.” Output encoding is the fix for cross-site scripting, covered next in this chapter. SQL injection is an input-parsing problem at the database layer and requires parameterized queries, not output encoding.

The MOVEit Transfer breach, 2023: One SQLi Flaw, Thousands of Victims

In late May 2023, the Cl0p ransomware group began exploiting CVE-2023-34362, a SQL injection vulnerability in Progress Software’s MOVEit Transfer. MOVEit is a managed file-transfer product widely used by corporations and government agencies to move sensitive files between organizations. The flaw let an unauthenticated attacker send a crafted SQL payload directly to MOVEit’s own web application, gaining access to its underlying database. From there, the attacker could plant a web shell that allowed arbitrary file theft from every organization running an unpatched instance.[1] Because MOVEit was itself infrastructure – a shared tool many separate companies used to transfer files, not a single company’s own application – one vulnerability in one product cascaded into one of the largest mass-breach events on record. Security researchers ultimately tied the campaign to more than 2,700 affected organizations and upwards of 95 million exposed individual records, including payroll data, health information, and Social Security numbers.[2] Many of those victims had never directly used MOVEit themselves; their data simply passed through a vendor who did. Cl0p did not need to compromise each of those thousands of organizations individually; a single unescaped input field in one shared product did the work for them. The case is the sharpest illustration in this chapter of why a parameterized-query fix at the root of the query, rather than any downstream filtering, is the only defense that actually closes an SQL injection flaw. It also shows why the severity of a single application vulnerability has to be evaluated against everyone who depends on that application, not just the organization that wrote it.

Cross-Site Scripting (XSS)

Definition: Cross-site scripting (XSS)

Including untrusted input in a page sent to a browser without neutralizing it first, so that HTML or JavaScript in that input executes with the same trust and access as code the developer actually wrote.

XSS is, structurally, the same root failure as SQL injection applied to a different interpreter: a failure to separate data from code. A string like <script>stealCookie(document.cookie)</script> stops being “just text” and becomes an executable instruction the moment a browser renders it unescaped. Because the script then runs inside the victim’s own already-authenticated session, it can read cookies and session tokens, make requests as the logged-in user, or silently redirect them – without needing to guess a single password.

Three variants matter, and distinguishing them is an AP-level skill in itself. Reflected XSS arrives in the request itself – typically a URL parameter – and the server echoes it straight back unescaped. The victim must be tricked into clicking a crafted link for the payload to fire at all; nothing is stored anywhere. Stored (persistent) XSS is saved server-side, in a comment field or user profile, and served to every subsequent viewer automatically. It is more dangerous precisely because it needs no social-engineering step: an administrator viewing an infected page may hand the attacker an elevated session without ever clicking anything crafted. DOM-based XSS lives entirely in client-side JavaScript: the page’s own script reads untrusted data from the URL fragment or document.referrer and writes it into an unsafe sink like innerHTML, never touching the server at all. That lets it hide from server-side filters completely, since the server never sees the malicious value.

The root defense against all three variants is the same regardless of which one is in play: output encoding, converting special characters (<, >, ", ') to their literal-text equivalents before untrusted data is placed into HTML. Modern frameworks (React, Vue, Django templates) escape by default, which is why the standard guidance is “always escape on output, not just on input.” The same string can be perfectly safe in a plain-text field and dangerous the moment it is rendered into HTML, so input validation alone cannot substitute for output-context escaping. A Content-Security-Policy header adds a second layer by restricting which scripts a browser will execute at all, and marking session cookies HttpOnly means JavaScript cannot read them even if a payload does manage to execute.

Common Misconception

“Filtering <script> tags is enough.” Attackers have countless other vectors – onerror=, javascript: URLs, SVG markup – and deny-listing a single tag name is trivially bypassed. Escaping output is the reliable fix; blocking specific tag names is not.

API Vulnerabilities

Modern applications are built almost entirely on APIs – structured, predictable endpoints a mobile app or web client calls directly, such as GET /api/accounts/482. That predictability is exactly what makes them an attractive target: an attacker does not need to reverse-engineer a page, only change a number in a URL and observe what comes back.

Definition: Broken Object Level Authorization (BOLA / IDOR)

An API flaw in which an endpoint accepts a client-supplied object identifier and returns or modifies that object without verifying that the currently authenticated caller is actually authorized for that specific object.

The failure is a confusion between two genuinely different questions. Authentication asks “is this token valid?”; authorization asks “does this specific token’s owner have permission to see this specific object?” BOLA is what happens when only the first question gets asked. A user logged in as account 482 who calls GET /api/accounts/483 on a BOLA-vulnerable API receives account 483’s private balance. The server verified only that a valid session existed, never whether that session belonged to account 483. Object identifiers like sequential database primary keys are frequently predictable, so this is easy for an attacker to discover by simply incrementing a number.

Two related flaws share the same root cause. Excessive data exposure happens when an endpoint returns a full internal object – password hashes, internal notes, another user’s email – because a developer serialized the entire database row and trusted the front-end to hide the extra fields. An attacker inspecting the raw response sees everything the front-end was merely choosing not to display. Broken function-level authorization occurs when an endpoint meant only for administrators, such as POST /api/users/482/promote, is reachable by any authenticated user because the server checks only that the caller is logged in, never that they hold the required role. APIs make this class of bug unusually common because access control has to be re-implemented correctly on every single endpoint. A large API surface with dozens or hundreds of endpoints multiplies the chance that one of them simply forgets the check.

Common Misconception

“Hiding a button in the UI prevents unauthorized access.” An attacker can call the API directly with a tool like curl or Postman, bypassing the UI entirely. Hiding a button changes what is convenient to do, not what is possible to do.

Data Breaches and Their Impact

Definition: Data breach

Any incident in which sensitive, protected, or confidential data is accessed, disclosed, or exfiltrated by someone not authorized to have it.

A breach can be caused by nearly any vulnerability in this chapter – SQL injection dumping a database, an XSS payload stealing session cookies, a BOLA flaw letting one account read another’s records. It can also come from an entirely non-technical cause: a lost laptop, a misconfigured cloud bucket left publicly readable, a successful phishing attack that hands over valid credentials. The cause varies widely; what makes it a breach specifically is that data crossed a trust boundary it should never have crossed.

Three factors determine severity, and reasoning about impact rather than mere occurrence is itself the AP-level skill here. Sensitivity of the exposed data ranges from a leaked list of public blog titles (low harm) through email addresses (enabling targeted phishing, but not immediately catastrophic) to PII, financial data, health records (PHI), and authentication secrets (high harm, enabling identity theft or account takeover outright). A breach exposing hashed-and-salted passwords is meaningfully less severe than one exposing the same passwords in plaintext, since hashing forces an attacker to do additional work before the data is usable. Scale multiplies both direct harm and organizational consequence: fifty records from an internal tool and fifty million records from a national retailer are not the same event by any measure that matters. Time to detect and contain determines how much data an attacker extracts and how much time they have to monetize it. Breaches an organization identifies and contains itself, quickly, cost markedly less on average than ones an attacker or an outside party discovers months later – a pattern IBM’s annual Cost of a Data Breach Report has found consistently for years[3] – which is exactly why the detection controls from Chapter 4.4 are properly understood as part of the breach-response story, not a separate topic.

Once a breach is confirmed, organizations follow a defined arc: contain (revoke credentials, patch the hole, isolate affected systems), assess (what data, how many records, which individuals), notify, and remediate (fix the root cause, rotate secrets, offer credit monitoring where appropriate). Many jurisdictions legally require notifying affected individuals and regulators within a set window – the European Union’s GDPR requires notifying the relevant supervisory authority within 72 hours of becoming aware of a breach, and is the canonical example.[4] Even a well-handled breach carries lasting cost beyond the immediate arc: leaked credentials frequently get reused elsewhere, since many people reuse passwords across sites, causing a second wave of unrelated account compromises long after the original incident is closed.

Common Misconception

“Small breaches don’t need a formal response.” A small breach of highly sensitive data – forty patients’ health records – can be more severe than a large breach of low-sensitivity data – four million newsletter email addresses. Severity depends on data type and scale together, never scale alone.

Check Your Understanding

1. A web application builds its database queries by directly concatenating user-supplied search terms without any validation. Which attack does this coding practice most directly enable?

Concatenating unvalidated user input into SQL queries allows an attacker to alter the query's logic, a SQL injection attack. Input sanitization and parameterized queries are the standard mitigations. CSRF, DDoS, and ARP spoofing exploit different mechanisms (session tokens, bandwidth, and link-layer resolution respectively) and are not caused by unvalidated SQL input.

2. When evaluating the risk of an application vulnerability, "likelihood" refers to which of the following?

Likelihood is the probability of a threat event occurring, how probable it is that an attacker will find and exploit a given vulnerability. Impact (choice B) is the other axis of risk evaluation. Record count (C) and patch time (D) may influence impact or remediation priority but are not the definition of likelihood.

3. A web application displays detailed database error messages to users whenever a query fails. Which risk does this behavior most directly create?

Verbose error messages often include table names, column names, and query fragments, exactly the reconnaissance information an attacker needs to craft a targeted injection payload. Production applications should suppress detailed errors and log them server-side only. The remaining choices do not describe consequences of error verbosity.

Review Questions

  1. Explain why parameterized queries prevent SQL injection structurally, while output encoding does not, and why the reverse is true for cross-site scripting.
  2. A comment field allows a payload to be saved and later viewed by an administrator with no link ever being clicked. Identify which XSS variant this is and explain why it is considered more dangerous than a variant requiring the victim to click a crafted link.
  3. An API endpoint checks that a request carries a valid authentication token but returns whatever object ID the client requests. Explain precisely which check is missing and why “the token is valid” is an insufficient basis for authorization.
  4. Explain why a breach exposing 40 patient health records can be scored as more severe than a breach exposing 4 million newsletter email addresses, using the severity factors from this chapter.
  5. Explain why hiding a UI button that would let a user access another account’s data does not constitute a real fix for a BOLA vulnerability, and describe what the actual fix must check and where.
  6. In the MOVEit Transfer breach, over 2,700 organizations were affected by a single SQL injection flaw in one shared product, including companies that never used MOVEit directly. Explain mechanically how an organization’s data could be exposed through a vendor’s vulnerability it had no direct relationship with, and why this changes how severity should be assessed compared to a breach confined to one company’s own application.
  7. Using the BOLA diagram, explain precisely why the authorize_request function in this chapter’s code listing cannot be satisfied by checking caller_id alone, and why the ownership_table lookup is the specific piece of logic a purely authentication-based check omits.

Answer Key

  1. Parameterized queries fix SQL injection structurally because the SQL template and the data values travel to the database driver on two separate channels – the driver never re-parses the data as syntax, no matter what characters it contains, so there is nothing left for an attacker to inject into. Output encoding cannot fix SQLi because the vulnerable parsing happens inside the database, before any HTML is ever generated. The relationship flips for XSS: the vulnerable parser is the browser’s HTML/JS engine, which runs long after the query already returned, so escaping the data at the point it is written into HTML removes its ability to be parsed as markup or script – while parameterizing a SQL query does nothing to protect a page that echoes that same data unescaped. 5.1.B
  2. This is stored (persistent) XSS. It is more dangerous than reflected XSS because it requires no social-engineering step at all – the payload sits in the database and fires automatically for every subsequent viewer, including an administrator who did nothing riskier than open the page, potentially handing the attacker an elevated session without a single crafted link ever being clicked. 5.1.B
  3. The missing check is object-level authorization: verifying that the specific authenticated caller is permitted to access the specific object identifier requested, not merely that the caller holds some valid token. “The token is valid” only answers the authentication question (who is this caller?); it says nothing about the authorization question (is this caller allowed to see this object?), and an API that asks only the first question is vulnerable to BOLA/IDOR the moment object identifiers are guessable or enumerable. 5.1.C
  4. Severity depends on sensitivity and scale together, not scale alone. Health records are high-sensitivity data – PHI – capable of enabling identity theft, insurance fraud, or targeted extortion against the specific 40 individuals involved, while newsletter email addresses are low-sensitivity data whose main downside is an increase in targeted phishing risk. Multiplying a high sensitivity weight by a small record count can therefore outscore a low sensitivity weight multiplied by a huge record count, exactly as the chapter’s severity model computes. 5.1.C
  5. Hiding a UI button only changes what is convenient to do through the graphical interface; it does nothing to the API endpoint itself, which remains reachable directly through a tool like curl or Postman that never renders the UI at all. The actual fix must check, on the server, for every request to that endpoint, that the object identifier supplied belongs to the authenticated caller – an ownership check performed in application logic before data is returned or modified, not a cosmetic change to what the front-end displays. 5.1.C
  6. MOVEit was infrastructure shared across organizations: many companies used it only to transfer files to or from other parties, meaning a third party’s own data could pass through, or be stored temporarily in, an unpatched MOVEit instance the affected organization never operated or even selected itself. The SQL injection flaw gave attackers direct database access and a web shell on the MOVEit server itself, so any file that server held – regardless of which downstream organization it belonged to – was reachable without the attacker ever touching that organization’s own network or systems. This changes severity assessment because impact can no longer be scoped to the vulnerable organization alone; it has to account for every party whose data transits or rests on the vulnerable shared system, which is exactly why one CVE produced harm at the scale of thousands of separate breach notifications. 5.1.A
  7. The function requires ownership_table.get(requested_object_id) == caller_id to return true, which is a comparison between the object’s actual owner and the requesting caller – information that authentication alone never supplies, since authentication only confirms the caller’s own identity, not who owns the specific object being requested. Checking caller_id alone (or merely that it is present and valid) answers only “is this caller someone?” – exactly the authentication branch in the diagram that passes cleanly even for account 483’s data being requested by account 482. The ownership_table lookup is precisely the missing authorization branch: it is the one piece of logic that actually asks whether this caller is allowed to reach this object, rather than merely confirming the caller is logged in as somebody. 5.1.B

Sources

  1. [1] Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency and Federal Bureau of Investigation, Cybersecurity Advisory AA23-158A, “#StopRansomware: CL0P Ransomware Gang Exploits CVE-2023-34362 MOVEit Vulnerability,” June 7, 2023.
  2. [2] Brett Callow, “Unpacking the MOVEit Breach: Statistics and Analysis,” Emsisoft Blog, figures as tallied June 28, 2024.
  3. [3] IBM Security, Cost of a Data Breach Report 2023.
  4. [4] Regulation (EU) 2016/679 (General Data Protection Regulation), Art. 33(1).

5.2 Linux Permission Controls

Every program and every privileged user of the system should operate using the least amount of privilege necessary to complete the job.

Jerome H. Saltzer and Michael D. Schroeder, “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems” (1975)

Motivation

Chapter 5.1 showed what happens when an application trusts input it should not. This chapter is about a different trust question: once a request is authenticated, how does a system decide what that identity is actually allowed to do? Every application, database, and operating system answers this with some access-control model. The three canonical models – owner- controlled, centrally labeled, and role-based – recur constantly in real systems, often blended together in the same deployment. The chapter then goes one level deeper into the model that dominates enterprise practice. It closes with what happens when a stolen credential meets a poorly designed access-control scheme: lateral movement across an entire network.

AP Course Alignment

Unit 5: Securing Applications and Data · Topic 5.2 – Protecting Applications and Data: Managerial Controls and Access Controls

  • 5.2.A Mitigate Risk Explain how two properties of a data item – the sensitivity band an organization has assigned it, and the state it happens to be in at the moment, whether resting on a disk, moving across a link, or open in a running process – together decide which protections apply to it and how strong those protections have to be.
  • 5.2.B Mitigate Risk Identify the controls that operate through policy and process rather than through code: classification schemes, named data owners, least privilege and separation of duties, periodic access review, acceptable-use and retention rules, and the vetting an organization applies to the software it buys.
  • 5.2.C Mitigate Risk Decide which access control model – discretionary, mandatory, role-based, or attribute-based – suits a given body of applications and data, given who must decide on access, how often the answer changes, and how much administrative effort the organization can sustain.
  • 5.2.D Mitigate Risk Put an access decision into effect on a Linux system: set ownership and group, choose a permission mode in octal or symbolic form, apply the special bits where they are warranted, control what new files inherit, and reach for a POSIX access control list when the three-class model cannot express the requirement.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

Data States and Classification

Two questions decide how much protection a piece of data deserves, and neither is technical. The first is what the data is – a marketing brochure and a payroll register are both, mechanically, just bytes on the same disk, but only one creates an obligation if it leaves the building. The second is where the data is at the moment you want to protect it, because the available mechanisms differ completely by answer.

Definition: The three states of data

Data at rest is written to persistent storage – a database file, a bucket object, a backup tape – and is protected by filesystem/object permissions and by storage encryption. Data in transit is crossing a network link and is protected by transport encryption: TLS for application traffic, IPsec or SSH tunnels host-to-host, including internal links. Data in use is loaded into memory and actively being processed, and generally must exist as plaintext for computation to occur. It is the hardest state to protect, addressed only by process isolation, memory zeroing after use, hardware enclaves, and, for narrow cases, homomorphic encryption.

Full-disk encryption is a clarifying example of why the states matter separately. It defends a powered-down, stolen laptop. But on a running server with the volume already unlocked, every process that can read the file reads plaintext, and the encryption contributes nothing at that point – access control and encryption are complementary controls, not substitutes for each other. Data in use is precisely the state the Heartbleed vulnerability (CVE-2014-0160, a 2014 buffer over-read flaw in OpenSSL) attacked.[1] The private keys it disclosed were properly encrypted at rest and properly protected in transit. They were read directly out of process memory in between.

A classification scheme assigns each data item to a sensitivity band so handling rules can be written once per band instead of once per file. Government practice uses Unclassified, Confidential, Secret, and Top Secret; commercial practice typically uses Public, Internal, Confidential, and Restricted. The label is administrative, not technical – nothing about the bytes of a Restricted file differs from a Public one. But it is what a policy attaches requirements to: this band may be stored only on encrypted volumes, may not leave a jurisdiction, must be retained seven years and then destroyed. Classification and state combine rather than substitute. A Restricted record at rest needs encrypted storage and a tightly scoped permission set. The same record in transit needs TLS with a modern cipher suite and enforced certificate validation. The same record in use needs process isolation and a guarantee the plaintext is never written to a log or crash dump. Aggregation means a collection can warrant a higher classification than any single member – one employee’s extension is Internal, the complete company directory is a social-engineering asset. The data lifecycle means classification is not permanent: an unreleased earnings figure is Restricted until filed, Public afterward. Data no longer needed should be destroyed on a retention schedule, since data that does not exist cannot be breached.

Controls are conventionally sorted by how they operate. Technical controls are enforced by machinery – a permission bit, a firewall rule, an encryption routine. Physical controls act on the material world – a locked rack, a badge reader, a shredder. Managerial (administrative) controls operate through policy, procedure, and human decision. They are what make the technical controls coherent: a permission bit answers “may this account read this file,” but only a managerial control answers “should this account exist at all, and who decided so.”

The managerial controls that bear directly on this chapter are: a data classification policy defining the bands; data ownership, distinguishing the owner who decides access, the custodian who operates storage on the owner’s behalf, and the user who works under the rules; and least privilege and separation of duties. Also directly relevant: periodic access review that catches privilege accumulation before it is exploited; onboarding/offboarding procedures governing when access is granted and revoked; change management ensuring a permission change is requested, approved, and recorded; training and acceptable-use policy; and vendor/third-party assessment extending the same expectations to software the organization did not write itself. Most well-known data losses were not defeated cryptography. They were an account that should have been closed, a grant nobody re-examined, or a production database copy that policy never forbade a developer from taking home.

Access Control Models

Definition: Access control

The policy and technical machinery that decides which subjects (users, processes, services) can perform which permissions (read, write, delete, execute) on which objects (files, database tables, API endpoints).

Discretionary Access Control (DAC) lets the owner of an object decide who else may access it – “discretionary” meaning the decision sits entirely with that owner. Traditional Unix file permissions are the textbook DAC system: whoever owns a file sets its read/write/execute bits for owner, group, and others, with no central authority approving the grant. DAC’s strength is flexibility – an owner can share access instantly without routing through an administrator – and its weakness is exactly the same fact viewed differently. Owners can carelessly over-grant, and nothing enforces an organization-wide minimum standard, so malware running as a user simply inherits and abuses whatever that user was permitted to touch. DAC also cannot enforce a rule about where data may go once it is read – nothing stops an authorized reader from copying a file’s contents into a new file they own and make world-readable.

Mandatory Access Control (MAC) removes discretion from the owner entirely. A central policy authority assigns labels to both subjects and objects – UNCLASSIFIED, SECRET, TOP SECRET – and a reference monitor compares the two on every access. MAC originated in government and military systems. Its classical formulation, the Bell–LaPadula model, gives two rules worth stating carefully because they are frequently reversed. No read up means a subject may not read an object above its clearance. No write down means a subject may not write to an object below its clearance – preventing a cleared user, or malware acting as that user, from copying Secret content into an Unclassified file.

SELinux and AppArmor are MAC’s Linux-world descendants, layered on top of the discretionary permission bits. That is why an SELinux denial can block an operation that ls -l says is permitted: both layers must allow it. MAC’s strength is enforcement no individual user can override; its weakness is administrative overhead, since labeling every object correctly requires careful policy design up front.

Definition: Role-Based Access Control (RBAC)

An access model where permissions attach to named roles rather than to individual users or sensitivity labels, and users inherit whatever permissions their assigned role or roles carry.

RBAC is today’s dominant enterprise model precisely because it scales: a hospital with five thousand staff might define thirty roles – Nurse, Billing Clerk, Auditor – rather than five thousand separate permission lists. When a nurse leaves and a replacement joins, an administrator reassigns one role rather than reconstructing an individual grant from scratch. RBAC also enforces separation of duties naturally, since a data-entry role can be kept structurally distinct from a data-approval role. Its failure mode is role proliferation: an organization that creates ever more fine-grained roles (“Junior Billing Clerk in Region 4”) recreates DAC’s original complexity problem in a different shape.

Definition: Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC)

An access model that evaluates a policy expression at request time over attributes of the subject, the object, the action, and the environment, rather than consulting a fixed list of grants or role assignments.

ABAC can express conditions the other three models cannot. It can permit a clinician to read a record only if four conditions all hold: the clinician is assigned to the patient’s care team; the record’s classification is at or below the clinician’s authorization; the request originates from a managed device on the hospital network; and the current time falls within the clinician’s shift. All four conditions are evaluated live, without inventing a distinct role for every combination – the role explosion that afflicts large RBAC deployments. The cost is that policies become software: they need authoring, testing, and version control, the attribute sources must stay authoritative and current, and a wrong attribute silently produces a wrong decision. NIST SP 800-162 is the standard reference for ABAC.[2]

In practice the models compose rather than compete. A single enterprise commonly runs RBAC for application entitlements, DAC on the filesystems those applications use, MAC through SELinux to confine daemons, and ABAC at the API gateway for context-dependent conditions. The design question is not which model is universally best but which decision each layer is being asked to make.

In practice, systems frequently blend all three: an operating system enforces MAC-style clearance labels overall while an application built on top of it manages job functions through RBAC, with DAC as the fallback for user-owned files. Cutting across every model is least privilege: a web server process reading a public directory should hold no write or delete permission at all, and an intern account should never reach salary tables. Least privilege is what caps the blast radius once any single account is compromised – an attacker who hijacks a low-privilege account inherits only that account’s narrow reach, not the whole system.

Common Misconception

“DAC is weak, so only MAC is secure.” Each model fits a different context. MAC’s overhead is impractical for ordinary consumer software; DAC is entirely appropriate when owners can be trusted to manage their own data reasonably.

Configuring Access Control on Linux
