Every file and directory on a Linux system carries an inode recording its owner, its group, and a compact permission field. The model divides potential accessors into exactly three classes – owner, group, and other (everyone else, including, on an internet-facing server, an unauthenticated web-server worker process). It tracks three independent bits per class: read (4), write (2), and execute (1). Running ls -l displays this as a ten-character string, e.g. -rw-r-----. The leading character is the file type (- regular file, d directory, l symlink). Each of the remaining three triads belongs to owner, group, and other in that order – a detail students routinely miscount by including the type character as part of the owner’s bits.

Two evaluation rules are easy to get wrong. First, the three triads are checked in order and the first match wins: if the requesting process is the file’s owner, only the owner triad is consulted. So a mode of 077 denies the owner everything while granting everyone else full access. Second, on a directory the bits mean something different from a file: r permits listing names inside it, w permits creating and deleting entries, and x (the search bit) permits traversing into it to reach a named child even without r. Because w on a directory governs the directory’s entry list rather than the files’ contents, holding write permission on a directory is enough to delete a file inside it that you cannot read and do not own. That is the exact problem the sticky bit (chmod +t, seen as t in /tmp’s mode) exists to solve, by restricting deletion inside a shared, world-writable directory to a file’s own owner. The related setuid and setgid bits make a program run with the privileges of its owning user or group rather than the invoking user’s. That makes them powerful, and consequently a classic privilege-escalation target when set on a file an attacker can also modify.

Permissions on a newly created file are not chosen by the creating program alone. The kernel computes the mode as the process’s requested mode (conventionally 666 for a file, 777 for a directory) with every bit set in the process’s umask cleared.

A umask is a default, not an enforcement mechanism – it decides what a file gets at birth, and any process may chmod afterward. It is also per-process and inherited, so setting it in an interactive shell has no effect on a service started by the system’s service manager. That service needs its own mask configured directly (e.g. UMask= in a systemd unit file).

The three-class model is intentionally coarse: it cannot express “grant write to exactly these two users but not the rest of the group,” nor different default permissions for different creators within one directory. A POSIX Access Control List (ACL), managed with getfacl and setfacl, layers arbitrarily many additional user- and group-specific grants on top of the base owner/group/other triad without replacing it. ACLs are additive, so the base bits are always evaluated too. A trailing + on an ls -l mode string signals that an ACL is present and the nine bits are not the whole story; auditing permissions without noticing it produces a confidently wrong conclusion.

Common Misconception

“If ls -l shows the permission, the operation is allowed.” On a system running SELinux or AppArmor, a MAC policy can still deny an operation the discretionary bits permit – both layers must agree, and a trailing . on the mode string signals an attached security context worth checking separately.

Roles, Permissions, and Least Privilege

A permission is a single narrow grant – “read the invoices table,” “restart the web service.” Real systems have hundreds or thousands of possible permissions, and assigning them one at a time to every individual user does not scale. It is slow, error-prone, and nearly impossible to audit after the fact (“does Dana still need delete-access to payroll from a project two years ago?”). RBAC’s roles are the answer – bundling permissions into named units a user is assigned as a whole, so a job change means reassigning a role rather than hand-editing a scattered permission list.

Why over-granting is silent and under-granting is loud

If a new employee’s role is missing a permission they actually need, they notice immediately and complain – the gap is loud and gets fixed fast. If a role carries a permission the employee will never use, nothing breaks and nobody notices – the excess is silent. This asymmetry is exactly why privilege creep accumulates unopposed. Over months and years, employees who change teams or take on temporary projects gain more and more permissions, because access is added when needed but almost never removed once it stops being needed. Removal requires someone to notice a gap that, by construction, never announces itself.

The security stake here is not tidiness – it is blast radius. A support agent’s role scoped tightly to ticket-handling permissions means a phished credential lets an attacker read and reply to tickets: contained, if unwelcome. The same account, after years of unchecked privilege creep, carrying delete_invoice and read_all_customer_pii because a manager once said “just give them access to be safe,” turns the identical phishing email into a much larger breach. Role review (recertification) is the deliberate counterweight: on a periodic schedule, managers or system owners must explicitly re-confirm that every person assigned to a role still needs it, and anything not re-confirmed is revoked.

Separation of duties reinforces least privilege structurally: the person who creates a vendor payment should never also be the person who approves it. That way, completing a fraud requires collusion between two people rather than the abuse of a single over-broad role. The most common design mistake here is a single giant “power user” role bundling every permission a manager might conceivably need across every function, rather than composing narrower roles (Approver, Report Viewer, Team Editor) assigned only as actually needed. It takes more setup effort up front, but it is the difference between answering “who can delete invoices?” by listing one role’s members versus manually auditing a thousand individual accounts.

Common Misconception

“Giving extra access ‘just in case’ is harmless if it’s never used.” Unused excess permission is exactly what increases blast radius the moment an account is compromised. It is a silent risk, not a neutral one, regardless of how rarely it is actually exercised.

Credential Theft and Lateral Movement

Most large breaches do not end where they begin. A phished marketing employee’s laptop usually has no direct access to a finance database or a domain controller – what makes a breach catastrophic is what happens next.

Definition: Lateral movement

An attacker using a compromised account or host to reach additional systems within a network after an initial foothold, typically by harvesting further credentials at each stop.

Credential theft is lateral movement’s starting fuel: once an attacker has one foothold, they harvest passwords, password hashes, or authentication tokens sitting in memory, configuration files, or the OS credential cache. Crucially, an attacker frequently does not need the plaintext password at all. Many enterprise authentication protocols accept hash-based or ticket-based authentication, so a stolen hash or session ticket can be replayed to authenticate as that user elsewhere without ever cracking it. This attack family is known as “pass-the-hash” or “pass-the-ticket.”

How least privilege stops lateral movement in its tracks

A stolen credential belonging to a normal user with narrow permissions stalls quickly – this is least privilege from earlier in this chapter doing exactly its job. If the compromised account is an IT administrator, or if the organization reuses one local-administrator password across every machine (a common and genuinely dangerous shortcut), the attacker instead hops from machine to machine nearly unopposed. The attacker harvests more credentials at each stop, hunting for a path to domain controllers, backup servers, or financial databases. This combination of lateral movement and privilege escalation expands an attacker’s reach both sideways and upward at once.

Several access-control practices from this chapter directly blunt this chain. Multi-factor authentication breaks the “stolen password equals access” assumption outright, since even a perfectly stolen credential is useless without the second factor, stopping the vast majority of credential-replay attempts cold. Credential hygiene – unique passwords per system, no shared local-admin password across machines, no plaintext credentials in scripts – removes the “one stolen credential unlocks everything” shortcut that fuels rapid lateral movement in the first place. Least privilege and network segmentation limit how far a single stolen credential can physically travel, exactly as Chapter 3.3 described.

Detection matters as much as prevention, because a sufficiently patient attacker will eventually find some foothold. Security teams watch for the same behavioral-anomaly signatures introduced in Chapter 3.5 and Chapter 4.4. Three signatures matter most: a single account authenticating to an unusually large number of distinct hosts in a short window; authentication at odd hours relative to the user’s normal pattern; or a user account suddenly invoking administrative tools it has never touched before.

Common Misconception

“A strong password alone stops credential theft.” Many lateral-movement techniques replay a stolen hash or ticket without ever needing the plaintext password at all. MFA and credential hygiene matter more here than password strength alone.

Check Your Understanding

1. Which of the following is an example of a managerial control related to application and data security?

Data classification policies are administrative (managerial) controls, they govern how data must be handled and protected through rules and procedures rather than technology. Firewalls, file permissions, and antivirus software are all technical controls enforced by hardware or software.

2. Which term correctly describes data that is stored on a server's hard drive and is not currently being transmitted or actively processed?

Data at rest refers to data stored on a medium, such as a hard drive, database, or backup tape, that is not actively moving over a network or being processed. The state of data determines which protective controls apply: encryption at rest, TLS in transit, memory protection in use.

3. An organization requires all developers to complete mandatory security-awareness training before being granted access to production systems. This requirement is best classified as which type of control?

Mandatory training is an administrative (managerial) control. It shapes behavior through policy and education rather than through hardware, software, or physical barriers. Managerial controls are preventive in nature but rely on human compliance to be effective.

Review Questions

  1. A government system enforces that a user cleared to SECRET cannot read a TOP SECRET document even if the document’s owner wants to share it. Identify which access control model this is and explain why DAC could not enforce this same guarantee.
  2. An RBAC system has a single “Manager” role bundling every permission a manager in any department might conceivably need. Explain why this violates least privilege even though every individual manager is, in fact, a manager.
  3. Explain why privilege creep is described as an asymmetric problem, using the loud-gap versus silent-excess distinction from this chapter.
  4. An attacker steals a password hash from a compromised laptop and uses it to authenticate to three other machines without ever learning the plaintext password. Explain why MFA would have stopped this specific attack while a stronger password policy alone would not have.
  5. Explain how least privilege and network segmentation work together to contain lateral movement, using the marketing-employee-versus-domain-admin scenario from this chapter.
  6. A file shows mode -rw-r----- and its owning directory is world-writable. Explain, using the directory-write rule from this chapter, how another user could delete this file despite having no permission bits on the file itself, and name the mechanism that exists specifically to prevent this in a shared directory.
  7. A hospital wants clinicians to read a patient record only if they are on that patient’s care team, on a managed device, and during their shift. Explain why RBAC cannot express this condition without role explosion, and name the model that can.
  8. Using the Bell–LaPadula diagram, explain why “no write down” is necessary in addition to “no read up” – specifically, describe an attack a system enforcing only no-read-up would remain vulnerable to.
  9. Malware executing under a SECRET-cleared user’s session attempts to copy a TOP SECRET file’s contents into a new UNCLASSIFIED file the malware creates. Explain which Bell–LaPadula rule blocks this, and why the block applies even though the malware is running with a legitimately cleared user’s own privileges rather than a stolen or escalated identity.
  10. An unreleased quarterly earnings figure is classified Restricted. Explain what protections it needs while at rest on a finance server, while in transit to the company’s outside auditors, and while open in a spreadsheet on an analyst’s laptop, and explain why the classification band alone does not tell you the answer to any of the three.

Answer Key

  1. This is Mandatory Access Control, applying Bell–LaPadula’s no-read-up rule. DAC could not enforce it because DAC leaves the access decision to the object’s owner, who could simply choose to share the document regardless of the requester’s clearance. MAC works precisely because the policy is set centrally and cannot be overridden by any individual owner’s discretion. 5.2.C
  2. Least privilege requires that an account hold only the access its actual function demands. Bundling every permission any manager anywhere might conceivably need into one role grants each individual manager far more access than their specific job requires. So a phished or malicious manager account has a blast radius sized to the whole bundled role rather than to that manager’s actual duties. 5.2.B
  3. A missing permission a new employee needs is loud: they notice immediately and report it, so it gets fixed quickly. An unused excess permission is silent: nothing breaks and nobody notices, so it persists indefinitely. Because access is added on demand but almost never removed without a deliberate review process, this asymmetry causes privilege to accumulate one-directionally over time. 5.2.B
  4. Pass-the-hash authentication replays the stolen hash directly without ever needing the plaintext password, so a stronger password policy changes nothing about how crackable the plaintext is – the attacker never attempts to crack it. MFA stops the attack because the stolen hash alone is insufficient to complete authentication without the second factor, which the attacker does not possess. 5.2.D
  5. Least privilege ensures a compromised marketing account’s credentials only unlock what a marketing job actually requires. Network segmentation ensures that even if the attacker tries to reach further, the finance database and domain controller sit on network segments the marketing host cannot route to in the first place. One control limits what the account is permitted to do, the other limits what hosts it can even reach, and together they contain lateral movement in two independent dimensions. 5.2.B
  6. Because the directory carries write permission for other, any user can create or delete entries in its listing regardless of the permissions on the individual files inside it. Deleting a file only requires write permission on its parent directory, not on the file itself. The sticky bit, set with chmod +t, exists specifically to prevent this in a shared directory by restricting deletion to a file’s own owner (or the directory’s owner, or root) even when the directory itself is world-writable. That is exactly why /tmp carries it by default. 5.2.D
  7. RBAC’s roles are static grants: a role either has a permission or it does not, independent of time, location, device, or the specific record in question. Expressing “only during this shift, only on a managed device, only for a patient on this clinician’s care team” would require a distinct role for every combination of those conditions – role explosion. Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC) evaluates such conditions live, as a policy expression over the request’s actual attributes, so it can express this rule directly without enumerating roles for every combination. 5.2.C
  8. A system enforcing only no-read-up still allows a subject cleared at SECRET to write freely into any object, including one labeled UNCLASSIFIED. A legitimately cleared user, or malware running under that user’s session, could read a SECRET or TOP SECRET file – permitted, since reading at or below one’s own clearance is always allowed. That user or malware could then write the contents into a new UNCLASSIFIED file. That effectively launders sensitive content down to a label anyone can read, with no rule in place to stop the write itself. No-write-down closes exactly this gap by forbidding a subject from writing to any object below its own clearance level. 5.2.C
  9. No-write-down blocks it: the malware is writing TOP SECRET content into an UNCLASSIFIED object, which is below the acting subject’s SECRET clearance regardless of what that content actually is. The block applies even though the malware runs under a legitimately cleared user’s own privileges, because Bell–LaPadula’s reference monitor evaluates every access by comparing labels. It does not evaluate whether the specific action is intentional or malicious. MAC’s entire strength is that it constrains what an account is permitted to do structurally, so it protects against a legitimate account behaving maliciously (through malware or otherwise) exactly as well as it protects against a mismatched external request. 5.2.C
  10. At rest, a Restricted item needs encrypted storage and a tightly scoped permission set that restricts read access to authorized users. In transit, it needs TLS with a modern cipher suite and enforced certificate validation. In use, it needs process isolation and a guarantee the plaintext is never written to a log or crash dump. The classification band alone only says how sensitive the data is; it says nothing about which specific technical mechanism applies, because that mechanism is determined by the item’s current state. Sensitivity band and current state have to be combined to decide both which protections apply and how strong they have to be. 5.2.A

Sources

  1. [1] National Vulnerability Database, CVE-2014-0160 (“Heartbleed”), U.S. National Institute of Standards and Technology, published April 7, 2014 – a buffer over-read in the TLS/DTLS heartbeat handling of OpenSSL 1.0.1 through 1.0.1f, allowing an attacker to read private keys and other sensitive data directly out of process memory.
  2. [2] Vincent C. Hu, David Ferraiolo, Richard Kuhn, et al., Guide to Attribute Based Access Control (ABAC) Definition and Considerations, NIST Special Publication 800-162, National Institute of Standards and Technology, originally published January 2014, updated August 2, 2019.

5.3 Symmetric Hashing and Key Storage

The system must not require secrecy, and it must be able to fall into the hands of the enemy without inconvenience.

Auguste Kerckhoffs, La Cryptographie Militaire (1883)

Motivation

Chapters 5.1 and 5.2 were about who is allowed to touch data. This chapter is about protecting the data itself, cryptographically, regardless of who eventually reaches it. Three questions organize it. When should stored data be reversible (encryption) versus permanently one-way (hashing)? How does symmetric encryption actually work, and how are its keys kept safe over their whole lifetime? And how do you prove not just that data is unaltered, but that it genuinely came from whoever claims to have sent it?

AP Course Alignment

Unit 5: Securing Applications and Data · Topic 5.3 – Protecting Stored Data with Cryptography

  • 5.3.A Mitigate Risk Decide whether data should be stored encrypted (recoverable) or hashed (one-way) based on whether the original value must ever be retrieved, and manage a symmetric key safely across its lifecycle – generation, storage, and rotation – so no single compromised secret exposes the underlying data.
  • 5.3.B Mitigate Risk Use a symmetric algorithm to encrypt and recover data, handling correctly the parts that surround the cipher itself – where the key comes from, how the initialization vector is generated, and how the ciphertext is authenticated so that tampering is detected rather than silently decrypted.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

Encryption versus Hashing

Both tools turn readable input into an unreadable-looking blob of bytes, and both are indispensable – but they solve opposite problems, and using the wrong one is a common, costly mistake.

Definition: Encryption

A reversible transformation of plaintext into ciphertext using a key; given the correct key, the ciphertext decrypts back to the exact original plaintext.

Definition: Hash function

A one-way function that maps an input of any size to a fixed-size digest, with no algorithm capable of reversing the process – given only a digest, the original input cannot be recovered, only guessed.

The question that separates them is simple: do you ever need to get the original back? A payroll file must be readable whenever payroll actually runs, so it is stored encrypted. A login password never needs to be recovered at all – the site only needs to know whether a submitted password matches what was registered – so it is stored hashed. At login, the input is hashed and the two digests are compared; the original password is never reconstructed anywhere in the process. If a site stored passwords encrypted instead, a database breach combined with key compromise would expose every user’s password in plaintext at once. Hashing removes that single point of failure entirely – there is no key to steal that would undo it.

Hash functions rely on the avalanche effect: two inputs differing by a single character produce completely unrelated digests, which is what makes a hash useful for detecting even the smallest tampering. But a naive hash of a common password produces the same digest every time it is hashed anywhere. That is exactly what rainbow tables – enormous precomputed password-to-hash lookup tables – exploit to crack common passwords instantly. A salt, a random value appended before hashing and stored alongside the digest, defeats this: even two users who chose the identical password end up with different digests, because their salts differ.

The SHA-2 family (SHA-256, SHA-512) is today’s standard for general-purpose hashing. MD5 and SHA-1 are deprecated for any security use because both have documented collision weaknesses – two genuinely different inputs producing the identical digest.[1] That collision weakness undermines the entire integrity guarantee a hash is meant to provide. For passwords specifically, purpose-built slow algorithms (bcrypt, scrypt, Argon2) are preferred over a fast general-purpose hash like SHA-256 alone, because their deliberate computational cost makes brute-force guessing expensive even on modern hardware.

Common Misconception

“Encoding (Base64) is a form of encryption.” Encoding is a lossless, keyless format change, trivially reversed by anyone – it provides zero confidentiality and should never be mistaken for either encryption or hashing.

Symmetric Encryption and Key Management

Definition: Symmetric encryption

A cryptographic system in which a single secret key performs both encryption and decryption; the same key must therefore be kept secret from anyone who should not read the protected data.

AES (Advanced Encryption Standard), established by NIST in 2001, is the modern industry standard, supporting 128-, 192-, and 256-bit keys, all currently considered secure against brute-force attack.[2] It replaced DES specifically because DES’s 56-bit key had become brute-forceable with modern hardware. AES is a block cipher: it encrypts fixed-size 128-bit blocks. Real data is almost always larger than one block, so a mode of operation defines how blocks chain together – and that choice matters enormously for security.

Why ECB mode leaks the exact shape of your data

Electronic Codebook (ECB) mode encrypts each block independently with the same key – which means two identical plaintext blocks always produce identical ciphertext blocks. Encrypt a bitmap image in ECB mode and the image’s outline remains clearly visible in the ciphertext. Large uniform regions of the source image become large uniform regions of ciphertext, in exactly the same shape. ECB should never be used for multi-block data regardless of how strong the underlying cipher is.

Cipher Block Chaining (CBC) fixes this by XORing each plaintext block with the previous ciphertext block before encrypting it, seeded by a random, non-secret Initialization Vector (IV) for the first block. The IV must be unique for every encryption – reusing one with the same key lets an attacker detect whether two messages start with identical data. GCM (Galois/Counter Mode) goes further: it turns the block cipher into a stream cipher and is an Authenticated Encryption with Associated Data (AEAD) mode. That means it provides confidentiality and integrity verification together through an authentication tag: if an attacker tampers with the ciphertext at all, decryption fails outright rather than silently producing corrupted plaintext.

A cryptographic system is only as secure as how it manages its keys across their whole lifecycle. Generation must use a CSPRNG (Cryptographically Secure Pseudo-Random Number Generator). An ordinary language’s built-in random module uses a predictable algorithm and must never generate a real key; Python’s secrets module or an OS entropy source like /dev/urandom is the correct choice. Storage must never mean hardcoding a key in source code or a plaintext configuration file, since either leaks the moment version control is compromised. Dedicated secrets managers or Hardware Security Modules (HSMs) are the correct alternative – both perform cryptographic operations internally, so a key never leaves the hardware in plaintext.

Large systems manage many keys through envelope encryption: a Data Encryption Key (DEK) encrypts the actual data, and a Key Encryption Key (KEK) encrypts (wraps) that DEK in turn. The wrapped DEK sits next to the encrypted data itself, while the KEK stays securely inside an HSM or secrets manager. To decrypt, the system sends the wrapped DEK to be unwrapped by the KEK, retrieves the plaintext DEK, and only then decrypts the actual data. Keys are rotated on a schedule to limit how much data any single compromised key would expose. When a key is finally retired, it is securely destroyed – cryptographic erasure – so that without it, the ciphertext it protected becomes permanently unrecoverable by design.

LastPass, 2022: When the Key Management Story Falls Apart

Attackers breached the password manager LastPass in two stages between August and October 2022, fully disclosed only in stages through December 2022. It became a widely cited case study in what happens when key-management assumptions do not hold up end to end.[3] In the first stage, attackers compromised a software engineer’s corporate laptop and used it to steal source code and technical secrets. In the second stage, using information from the first, attackers targeted a senior DevOps engineer, ultimately capturing credentials that gave access to cloud storage backups containing customer vault data. Those vaults were encrypted, meant to be readable only by each customer, using a key derived from that customer’s own master password – which LastPass itself never stored. For most customers, that design held: without the master password, the stolen vaults were not directly readable. But LastPass’s own later disclosure revealed two compounding weaknesses. Some vaults had been created years earlier under a much lower password-based key-derivation iteration count than the company’s current standard. Older accounts had not always been migrated forward, leaving them meaningfully cheaper for an attacker to brute-force offline. And within each vault, some fields – website URLs among them – had historically been stored unencrypted for functional reasons, exposing which sites a customer had accounts on even without cracking anything. The lesson generalizes past this one company. Envelope encryption and strong algorithms only deliver the protection this chapter describes when every parameter in the chain is actually current for every record – not just the ones created after a policy improved. That chain includes the key-derivation iteration count, which fields are actually encrypted, and whether older data was ever upgraded to current standards. A cryptographic design can be correct on paper and still fail in exactly the records an organization forgot to re-encrypt.

Common Misconception

“AES-ECB is secure because AES is strong.” Even with a maximally strong cipher, ECB mode encrypts identical plaintext blocks into identical ciphertext blocks, exposing data patterns regardless of how unbreakable the underlying AES computation itself is.

Integrity and Authenticity

A plain hash lets you detect whether data changed by accident – a bad disk sector, a dropped byte in transit – but it does nothing against an intelligent attacker. Hash functions are public and keyless: anyone, including the attacker, can compute SHA-256 over any input they choose. An attacker who intercepts a message and its hash, edits the message, and recomputes the hash over the tampered content produces a digest that matches perfectly. The check passes cleanly, because the attacker computed it correctly over content they altered.

Definition: MAC (Message Authentication Code)

A keyed hash that proves both integrity (the data was not altered) and authenticity (the data genuinely came from whoever holds the shared secret key). A plain hash, by contrast, can only ever demonstrate the first property – and only over a channel an attacker cannot also tamper with.

The obvious first attempt at building one, H(key || message), has a real vulnerability in any hash built on the Merkle-Damgard construction, which includes MD5, SHA-1, and SHA-256. These functions expose their entire internal state as the output digest. That means an attacker who knows H(key || message) can compute a valid hash for key || message || padding || attacker_data without ever learning the key – a length-extension attack. The digest already tells the attacker exactly what internal state the function was in after processing the original input, letting them resume hashing from there.

Definition: HMAC

The NIST FIPS 198-1 standardized keyed-hash construction,[4] \(\mathrm{HMAC}(K, m) = H\big((K \oplus \mathrm{opad}) \,\|\, H((K \oplus \mathrm{ipad}) \,\|\, m)\big)\), which nests the hash function twice around two independently derived keyed prefixes so that no exposed intermediate state is ever available to extend.

Because the final output is a hash of a hash, with the key folded in on both passes, an attacker who only ever sees the final tag has no usable internal state to extend. They cannot produce a valid tag for any message without the key itself. The nesting is what makes HMAC provably resistant to length extension, even while built directly on a vulnerable Merkle-Damgard hash like SHA-256.

HMAC shows up constantly in practice. In API request signing, a client and server share a secret, and every request carries a tag over its contents so the server can reject anything tampered with or forged. In webhook verification, a SaaS platform signs a payload with HMAC-SHA256 so the receiving server can confirm the request really came from that platform. And JWT tokens using the HS256 algorithm have a server-side secret sign the header and payload, so tampering with a token’s own claims is detectable. In every case, HMAC answers the same question: did this data arrive unmodified, from someone who actually holds the shared secret? That is a question a plain hash structurally cannot answer, once an attacker is capable of rewriting both the message and its accompanying digest.

Common Misconception

“A matching hash proves the message wasn’t tampered with.” Only if the attacker could not also recompute the hash after tampering. A plain, keyless hash gives no such guarantee the moment an attacker can rewrite both the message and its accompanying digest together.

Check Your Understanding

1. Which of the following is an example of a security control that protects data at rest?

Full-disk encryption protects data at rest by rendering the stored contents unreadable without the decryption key. Firewall rules and IDS protect data in transit or detect network-layer events; a VPN protects data in motion between sites. None of the other options act on data while it is stored on a device.

2. Which of the following best describes the defining characteristic of a symmetric encryption algorithm?

Symmetric encryption algorithms, such as AES and 3DES, use a single shared secret key for both the encryption and decryption operations. Option A describes asymmetric encryption (e.g., RSA). Option B describes a cryptographic hash function. Option D describes a secret-sharing scheme, which is a separate concept.

3. A security analyst wants to verify that a stored configuration file has not been altered since it was last saved by an authorized administrator. Which cryptographic control is most appropriate for this specific purpose?

A cryptographic hash function produces a fixed-length digest of the file's contents. Any subsequent modification to the file, however small, changes its hash value. By comparing the stored hash against a freshly computed hash, an analyst can detect unauthorized changes. Encryption provides confidentiality, not integrity verification. A digital certificate authenticates identity. A decryption key alone does not verify file integrity.

Review Questions

  1. A company needs to run payroll, which requires reading employee salary figures, and also needs to verify user login passwords. Explain which cryptographic control belongs to each task and justify the choice using the “do you need the original back” test from this chapter.
  2. Explain why encrypting a bitmap image in ECB mode leaves the image’s outline visible in the ciphertext, and identify the specific property of ECB mode responsible for this leak.
  3. An organization hardcodes an AES key directly in its application source code, committed to a private Git repository. Explain why this is considered unsafe key storage even though the repository is private, and describe the correct alternative.
  4. An attacker intercepts a message and its SHA-256 hash, edits the message, and recomputes a matching hash over the tampered content. Explain why the recipient’s integrity check passes despite the tampering, and explain what HMAC adds that would have caught it.
  5. Explain why H(key || message) is vulnerable to a length-extension attack on SHA-256, and explain specifically how HMAC’s double-nested construction closes that gap.
  6. In the LastPass breach, most customer vaults remained unreadable to the attackers because the decryption key was derived from a master password LastPass never stored. Explain why older vaults created under a lower key-derivation iteration count were still meaningfully more exposed than newer ones, even though both used the identical envelope-encryption design in principle.
  7. Using the envelope-encryption diagram, explain why an organization can rotate its KEK on a regular schedule without re-encrypting its entire dataset of stored files. Identify exactly which artifact actually changes when a KEK rotation happens.

Answer Key

  1. Payroll requires the original salary figures to be recoverable whenever payroll actually runs, so it is stored encrypted – the “do you need the original back” answer is yes. Login passwords never need to be recovered; the system only needs to confirm a submitted password matches what was registered, so passwords are stored hashed. The original is never reconstructed anywhere in the verification process. 5.3.A
  2. ECB mode encrypts each fixed-size block independently under the same key, so two identical plaintext blocks always produce identical ciphertext blocks. Large uniform regions of a bitmap image consist of many identical or near-identical blocks, which map to identical ciphertext blocks in exactly the same arrangement, so the image’s outline survives encryption intact. The specific property responsible is ECB’s lack of chaining or randomization between blocks. 5.3.B
  3. A private repository is not a secrecy boundary that survives normal operations: any developer with repository access, any CI system that clones it, and anyone who obtains a compromised credential or a leaked clone can read the key. The key also remains exposed in the repository’s full history even after later removal. The correct alternative is a dedicated secrets manager or Hardware Security Module, which stores the key outside source control. In the HSM case, it also performs cryptographic operations internally, so the key never leaves the hardware in plaintext at all. 5.3.A
  4. The check passes because SHA-256 is a public, keyless function: the attacker can compute a correct hash over any content they choose, including their own tampered message. The recomputed hash matches the tampered content perfectly. HMAC adds a shared secret key folded into the computation itself, so an attacker without that key cannot produce a valid tag for their tampered message, even though they can still compute an ordinary SHA-256 hash of it. 5.3.B
  5. H(key || message) is vulnerable because Merkle-Damgard hash functions expose their entire internal state as the output digest. An attacker who knows H(key || message) already knows exactly what internal state the function was in after processing the original input. They can resume hashing from there, computing a valid hash over key || message || padding || attacker_data without ever learning the key. HMAC closes this by nesting the hash function twice around two independently keyed prefixes, so the final tag is a hash of a hash with the key mixed in on both passes. An attacker who sees only the final tag has no exposed intermediate state usable as a starting point for extension. 5.3.B
  6. A key-derivation function’s iteration count directly controls how computationally expensive each single guess is for an attacker performing offline brute-force against the master password. That is the same deliberate-slowness principle bcrypt and Argon2 apply to password hashing more generally. A vault created under a much lower iteration count requires far less computation per guess, so the identical envelope-encryption design and identical algorithm still yield a dramatically cheaper attack surface for older vaults. The design’s security depends on a tunable parameter that was never uniformly current across every account, not on the algorithm choice alone. 5.3.A
  7. Because the KEK only wraps the (much smaller) DEK rather than touching the actual data directly, rotating the KEK requires only unwrapping every existing DEK with the old KEK and re-wrapping it with the new one. That is a small, fast operation compared to re-encrypting the underlying files themselves, which can be arbitrarily large and numerous. The artifact that actually changes during a KEK rotation is the wrapped DEK stored alongside each piece of encrypted data. The encrypted data itself, and the DEK’s own plaintext value, never need to be touched at all. 5.3.B

Sources

  1. [1] National Institute of Standards and Technology, “NIST Transitioning Away from SHA-1 for All Applications,” NIST Computer Security Resource Center, December 15, 2022.
  2. [2] National Institute of Standards and Technology, Federal Information Processing Standards Publication 197, Advanced Encryption Standard (AES), November 26, 2001.
  3. [3] LastPass, “Security Incident Update & Recommended Actions,” The LastPass Blog, March 1, 2023 (final incident report, consolidating the August–December 2022 disclosures).
  4. [4] National Institute of Standards and Technology, Federal Information Processing Standards Publication 198-1, The Keyed-Hash Message Authentication Code (HMAC), July 16, 2008.

5.4 Asymmetric Cryptography

We stand today on the brink of a revolution in cryptography.

Whitfield Diffie and Martin Hellman, “New Directions in Cryptography” (1976)

Motivation

Symmetric encryption in Chapter 5.3 has an uncomfortable bootstrap problem: both parties need the same secret key before they can talk securely. So how do they share it the first time, over a network anyone might be listening to? This chapter’s answer is asymmetric cryptography: two mathematically linked keys, one of which can be handed to literally anyone without weakening security at all. The chapter builds from the key pair itself, through the specific algorithm (RSA) that makes it work, to digital signatures. It closes with the infrastructure that lets you trust a stranger’s public key really belongs to them in the first place.

AP Course Alignment

Unit 5: Securing Applications and Data · Topic 5.4 – Asymmetric Cryptography

  • 5.4.A Mitigate Risk Use a public/private key pair correctly for the operation actually intended – encrypting to a recipient’s public key for confidentiality, or signing with one’s own private key for authenticity – recognizing that the two operations are not reversible readings of one another.
  • 5.4.B Analyze Risk Compare key sizes across algorithm families on a common footing (security strength in bits, per NIST SP 800-57) rather than assuming equal key length means equal security, and reason about what a given key size still protects against today’s best-known attacks.
  • 5.4.C Mitigate Risk Establish trust in a stranger’s public key through a certificate and a chain of trust anchored in a pre-installed root, and evaluate whether a presented certificate should actually be trusted.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

Public/Private Key Pairs

Definition: Asymmetric cryptography

A cryptographic system built on two mathematically linked keys – a public key that can be freely distributed, even posted publicly, and a private key kept secret by its owner forever – where what one key does, only its partner can undo.

Key generation starts from a hard mathematical problem: easy to compute in one direction, computationally infeasible to reverse without special knowledge. For RSA, that problem is integer factorization – multiplying two large primes is fast, but recovering them from only their product is not, for large enough primes. Two large primes \(p\) and \(q\) are chosen; their product \(n = p \times q\) becomes part of both keys. A public exponent \(e\) is chosen (nearly always 65537 in practice). A private exponent \(d\) is then derived such that \(e \times d \equiv 1 \pmod{\phi(n)}\), where \(\phi(n) = (p-1)(q-1)\) – a value that can only be computed efficiently if you already know \(p\) and \(q\). The public key is \((e, n)\); the private key is \((d, n)\).

The keys are asymmetric in function, not merely in secrecy, and this produces two entirely separate use cases. Encrypt with the public key; decrypt with the private key: if Alice wants to send Bob a secret, she encrypts with Bob’s public key. Only Bob’s private key can reverse it – not even Alice, who did the encrypting, can decrypt her own message afterward. This is confidentiality. Sign with the private key; verify with the public key: if Bob wants to prove he authored something, he signs with his own private key. Anyone holding his public key can then verify it, knowing only Bob could have produced a signature that checks out. This is authenticity, covered fully later in this chapter.

The one-way trapdoor

A useful mental model is a trapdoor in a floor: easy to fall through in one direction, impossible to climb back through without the special mechanism. Multiplying \(p\) and \(q\) is the easy fall; factoring \(n\) back into \(p\) and \(q\) is the locked climb. The private key is the trapdoor mechanism that makes the reverse direction possible for its holder alone. Other asymmetric systems use a different one-way problem entirely – discrete logarithms for Diffie-Hellman, elliptic-curve discrete logarithms for ECC – but the trapdoor structure recurs universally.

Security scales with key size, since larger keys make the underlying factoring problem harder. But key length is not comparable across algorithm families: a 256-bit AES key, a 256-bit ECC key, and a 256-bit RSA modulus describe three completely different security levels, because each family faces a different best-known attack. The unit that is comparable is security strength, measured in bits, where a scheme with \(s\) bits of strength requires about \(2^s\) operations to break. Table 2 of NIST SP 800-57 Part 1 Rev. 5[1] gives the mapping between families:

The RSA column explodes while the other two grow roughly linearly because of the shape of the best attack against each. Against a well-chosen symmetric cipher or elliptic curve, the best known attack is generic – exhaustive search for AES, Pollard’s rho for ECC, which costs about the square root of the group order. So one more bit of key buys one more bit of strength for AES, and roughly half a bit for ECC. RSA instead faces the General Number Field Sieve, whose running time is sub-exponential in the modulus size. So the next 16 bits of strength cost not a few more bits of modulus but thousands: 2048 to 3072 bits for the step from 112 to 128 bits of strength, and 3072 to 15360 bits for the step from 128 to 256. RSA private-key operations also cost roughly the cube of the modulus size, so a 15360-bit RSA key is not merely large to transmit but unusable at scale, while a 512-bit elliptic curve remains cheap. That cost curve is why modern TLS and SSH deployments default to ECDSA P-256 or Ed25519 rather than to ever-larger RSA keys. It is also why an RSA-1024 key, offering only about 80 bits of strength, sits below the security floor NIST withdrew support for in 2013.[2] None of this matters, however, unless you actually receive the genuine public key belonging to whoever you want to talk to, rather than one an attacker substituted – the trust problem this chapter closes with in its final section.

Common Misconception

“If I encrypt with the private key, it is more secure.” Encrypting with the private key provides no confidentiality at all, since anyone holding the matching public key can decrypt it. Signing with the private key is for authenticity, a completely different guarantee from secrecy.

The RSA Algorithm

Everything RSA does reduces to one operation, applied twice: modular exponentiation. Encryption computes \(c = m^e \bmod n\); decryption computes \(m = c^d \bmod n\). A theorem from number theory (built on Euler’s theorem) guarantees \((m^e)^d \bmod n = m\) whenever \(d\) was derived correctly relative to \(e\), \(p\), and \(q\). Encrypting and then decrypting always returns the original message, but only for whoever holds the matching \(d\).

A complete worked example, with nothing simplified away

Key generation. Choose two primes \(p = 61\) and \(q = 53\). The modulus is \(n = pq = 3233\). Euler’s totient is \(\phi(n) = (p-1)(q-1) = 60 \times 52 = 3120\). Choose a public exponent coprime to \(\phi(n)\): take \(e = 17\), since \(\gcd(17, 3120) = 1\). The private exponent \(d\) is the modular inverse of \(e\) modulo \(\phi(n)\), i.e. the value satisfying \(ed \equiv 1 \pmod{3120}\). The extended Euclidean algorithm gives \(d = 2753\), which checks: \(17 \times 2753 = 46801 = 15 \times 3120 + 1\). The public key is \((e,n) = (17, 3233)\); the private key is \((d,n) = (2753, 3233)\). The primes \(p\) and \(q\) themselves must now be destroyed or protected exactly as carefully as \(d\), since either one immediately yields \(d\).

Encryption. To send \(m = 65\), a sender computes \(c = m^e \bmod n\) using the recipient’s public key. Doing this by square-and-multiply, so every intermediate value can be checked by hand:

\[65^1 \bmod 3233 = 65, \quad 65^2 \bmod 3233 = 992, \quad 65^4 \bmod 3233 = 1232,\]
\[65^8 \bmod 3233 = 1547, \quad 65^{16} \bmod 3233 = 789.\]

Since \(17 = 16 + 1\), \(65^{17} = 65^{16} \times 65^1 \equiv 789 \times 65 = 51285 \equiv 2790 \pmod{3233}\). The ciphertext is \(c = 2790\).

Decryption. The recipient recovers \(m = c^d \bmod n = 2790^{2753} \bmod 3233 = 65\) using their private key – an exponent large enough that it is checked by machine rather than by hand. Notice that running the same pair of operations in the opposite order, private key first and public key second, is the skeleton of a signature rather than of an encryption. The direction of the two keys, not the arithmetic itself, is what distinguishes the two use cases.

Real implementations almost universally use \(e = 65537\) (\(2^{16}+1\)) rather than a small arbitrary prime, for three reasons. It is prime, so \(\gcd(e, \phi(n)) = 1\) is essentially guaranteed for random large \(p, q\). Its binary form (a single 1 bit, fifteen 0 bits, a final 1 bit) makes repeated-squaring exponentiation fast. And it is large enough to defeat low-exponent attacks that break RSA when \(e\) is very small (such as \(e=3\)) and the same message is sent unpadded to multiple recipients. Why this resists reversal without \(d\) traces directly back to Chapter 5.4’s first section: computing \(d\) from \((e, n)\) alone requires \(\phi(n)\), which requires factoring \(n\) into \(p\) and \(q\). That is trivial for a toy \(n=33\), but computationally infeasible for a real 2048-bit modulus with no known classical algorithm. The asymmetry that protects RSA lives entirely in the difficulty of factoring, never in the exponentiation itself, which is fast in both directions once the correct exponent is known.

Common Misconception

“Small numbers in the worked example mean RSA is easy to break.” The arithmetic is identical at any size; security comes from using primes hundreds of digits long, where factoring \(n\) becomes computationally infeasible – not from the operations themselves becoming more complex.

Digital Signatures

A digital signature is the asymmetric analogue of the HMAC from Chapter 5.3. Where HMAC relies on one shared secret both parties possess, a signature uses a key pair: only the signer holds the private key, and anyone at all can hold the public key. That structural difference unlocks a guarantee HMAC cannot offer.

Signing the entire message directly with RSA’s private-key operation is both slow (RSA exponentiation cost grows with input size) and structurally risky. An attacker who obtains signatures on certain chosen messages can sometimes combine them algebraically to forge a signature on a message never actually signed. Real schemes fix both problems by never signing the message itself:

The signer computes a fixed-size digest of the message with a cryptographic hash, and signs that digest rather than the message. A verifier recomputes the hash independently, recovers the digest the signature actually encodes, and checks the two match. Hashing first keeps the signature a fixed, small size regardless of message length, and runs the expensive RSA step only once. It also inherits the avalanche effect from Chapter 5.3: a single changed bit in the message produces a completely different hash, so any tampering is caught the instant verification is attempted.

A signature that verifies successfully gives three distinct guarantees at once. Authenticity – the signature could only have been produced by whoever holds the matching private key. Integrity – because the signature covers a hash of the message, any post-signing modification changes that hash and breaks verification immediately. Non-repudiation – the signer cannot credibly deny having signed, since nobody else possesses their private key.

Why signatures beat HMAC for this specific guarantee

HMAC gives integrity and authenticity between exactly two parties who share a secret key, but no non-repudiation at all. If Alice and Bob share an HMAC key and Alice receives a validly tagged message, she knows it came from “Bob or herself,” since either party holding the shared secret could have produced that exact tag. There is no way to prove to a third party which of the two actually created it. A digital signature removes this ambiguity structurally, because signing and verifying use different keys: only the signer can produce a valid signature, but anyone can verify one. That also lets a single public key scale to an unlimited number of verifiers, unlike HMAC, where every new verifier would need its own shared secret – and could then forge tags for everyone else.

Common Misconception

“A digital signature encrypts the message for confidentiality.” It provides no secrecy at all; the message is frequently sent in the clear alongside its signature. Signing proves authenticity and integrity, never confidentiality – that requires separately encrypting with the recipient’s public key.

PKI and Digital Certificates

The previous section left a bootstrap problem deliberately unsolved: asymmetric cryptography lets you hand out your public key freely, but only helps if the recipient can be sure that key genuinely belongs to who it claims. If an attacker slips you a different public key while claiming to be your bank, every guarantee from this chapter collapses at once – you would encrypt your password with the attacker’s key, believing it protected.

Definition: Digital certificate

A small, signed document binding a public key to an identity, containing at minimum a subject (the identity vouched for), the subject’s public key, an issuer (who is vouching), a validity period, and a digital signature over all of those fields, produced with the issuer’s own private key.

That signature is exactly the sign/verify operation from the previous section: the issuer hashes the certificate’s fields and signs the digest. Anyone holding the issuer’s public key can then verify that the certificate has not been altered since the issuer vouched for it. The issuer here is a Certificate Authority (CA), but this only pushes the trust question up one level: why trust the CA’s own public key?

Definition: Chain of trust

The sequence of signed certificates from a leaf certificate up through intermediate CAs to a root CA, whose self-signed certificate is pre-installed in an operating system or browser’s trust store, verified out-of-band by the OS vendor rather than by the individual user.

A root CA rarely signs end-entity certificates directly – its private key is kept extremely well protected, often entirely offline – and instead signs intermediate CA certificates, delegating signing authority downward. The intermediate CA then signs the actual leaf certificate presented to a browser. Validating this chain means verifying each certificate’s signature using the next certificate’s public key – the leaf against the intermediate’s key, the intermediate against the root’s. The validator then confirms the chain terminates at a root already present in the trust store.

Beyond the signature chain, a client checks each certificate’s validity window – one presented before not_before or after not_after is rejected, which is why an expired certificate throws a browser warning. A client also checks its revocation status, since a certificate’s stated expiration might be years away while the situation that made it trustworthy can change immediately, most commonly because its private key was stolen. A CA cannot un-sign an already-issued certificate, so it instead publishes revocation through a Certificate Revocation List (CRL), a periodically updated, CA-signed list of revoked serial numbers. Or it publishes through OCSP, where a client asks a CA-operated responder in real time whether one specific certificate is still good and receives a signed answer without downloading an entire list.

Common Misconception

“Revocation and expiration are the same thing.” Expiration is a scheduled, built-in end to a certificate’s validity window. Revocation is an unscheduled, active withdrawal of trust before that date, typically because the private key was compromised in the meantime.

Check Your Understanding

1. A LightningGrid security engineer wants to send a confidential report to a colleague at another plant using PGP. Which key should the engineer use to encrypt the message?

In asymmetric encryption, a message intended for a specific recipient is encrypted using that recipient's public key. Only the recipient's corresponding private key, never shared with anyone, can decrypt it, ensuring confidentiality.

2. A LightningGrid engineer digitally signs a classified message before transmitting it. Which key is used to create the digital signature?

Digital signatures are created using the sender's private key. The recipient verifies the signature using the sender's public key, confirming both authenticity (the message came from the claimed sender) and non-repudiation (the sender cannot later deny sending it).

3. A LightningGrid security team is selecting between 1024-bit and 3072-bit RSA keys for protecting inter-plant communications. Which statement best describes the effect of choosing the longer key?

RSA security depends on the hardness of factoring large semiprime numbers. Longer keys increase the size of that number exponentially, making factoring vastly more difficult. 1024-bit RSA is now considered insufficient against modern factoring attacks; 3072-bit is a current recommended minimum for long-term security.

Review Questions

  1. Alice encrypts a message with Bob’s public key and sends it. Explain why Alice herself cannot decrypt the ciphertext afterward, and explain what property of the key pair makes this true.
  2. Using the worked RSA example in this chapter (\(p=61\), \(q=53\), \(e=17\), \(d=2753\), \(n=3233\)), explain in your own words why encrypting \(m=65\) and then decrypting the result returns exactly \(65\), referencing the relationship between \(e\), \(d\), and \(\phi(n)\).
  3. Explain why real signature schemes hash a message before signing it, rather than signing the raw message directly with RSA, citing both a performance reason and a structural-security reason from this chapter.
  4. Two colleagues, Alice and Bob, share an HMAC key. Alice receives a validly tagged message and cannot prove to a third party whether Bob or she herself produced it. Explain why switching to digital signatures resolves this specific ambiguity.
  5. A browser encounters a self-signed certificate for a public website and rejects it, while the same browser accepts a different self-signed certificate manually installed on a private internal network. Explain why the identical cryptographic construction produces two different trust outcomes.
  6. Using the chain-of-trust diagram, explain what specifically breaks validation if an intermediate CA’s certificate is presented but the leaf certificate’s issuer field does not match the intermediate’s subject field, even if every individual signature in the chain is otherwise mathematically valid.
  7. A root CA’s private key is compromised, but the root CA itself has not yet issued a revocation or informed browser vendors. Explain why the security strength table earlier in this chapter (comparing AES, RSA, and ECC key sizes) is irrelevant to assessing the danger here, and identify what actually determines the scope of damage.

Answer Key

  1. Alice cannot decrypt her own ciphertext because encryption with Bob’s public key can only be reversed by Bob’s matching private key, which only Bob holds – not even the party that performed the encryption. This follows from the defining property of the key pair: what one key does, only its mathematically linked partner can undo, and Alice possesses neither Bob’s private key nor any shortcut around needing it. 5.4.A
  2. Because \(d\) was derived so that \(ed \equiv 1 \pmod{\phi(n)}\) (here \(17 \times 2753 \equiv 1 \pmod{3120}\)), a theorem built on Euler’s theorem guarantees \((m^e)^d \bmod n = m\) for any \(m\) coprime to \(n\). Concretely, \(65^{17} \bmod 3233 = 2790\), and \(2790^{2753} \bmod 3233\) returns exactly \(65\) again, because the modular exponentiation with \(d\) is mathematically constructed to be the exact inverse operation of exponentiation with \(e\) under modulus \(n\). 5.4.A
  3. The performance reason: RSA exponentiation cost grows with the size of the input, so signing a short, fixed-size hash rather than an arbitrarily long message keeps the expensive private-key operation cheap and constant-size regardless of message length. The structural-security reason: an attacker who obtains RSA signatures on certain chosen raw messages can sometimes combine them algebraically to forge a signature on a message never actually signed. Hashing first removes this algebraic structure by feeding the signature operation an unpredictable, avalanche-effect digest instead of the raw message. 5.4.A
  4. HMAC’s shared-secret key does not distinguish which of the two key-holders produced a given tag, since either party could have computed it – there is no third piece of evidence separating Alice from Bob. Digital signatures use different keys for signing and verifying: only the actual signer’s private key can produce a valid signature. So a signature that verifies against Bob’s public key could only have come from Bob, resolving the ambiguity structurally rather than through trust between the two parties. 5.4.A
  5. Trust in a self-signed certificate depends entirely on whether its signing key is already present in the verifier’s trust store through some independent, out-of-band decision. A public website’s self-signed certificate is not signed by any CA in the browser’s pre-installed trust store, so the chain of trust cannot be validated and the browser rejects it. The internal network’s self-signed certificate was manually installed into that specific browser’s trust store by an administrator, which is exactly the out-of-band act of trust a root CA normally provides at OS-vendor scale. So the identical cryptographic construction is accepted there and nowhere else. 5.4.C
  6. Validation requires each certificate’s stated issuer to match the subject of the certificate that actually signed it, forming an unbroken chain of names as well as an unbroken chain of signatures. If the leaf’s issuer field does not match the intermediate’s subject field, the chain link itself is broken – there is no defined next certificate to verify the leaf’s signature against, regardless of whether some other, unrelated certificate’s signature happens to be mathematically valid. A validator following the chain-of-trust logic returns a broken-chain-link failure at exactly this point, and never reaches the question of cryptographic validity for the mismatched link at all. 5.4.C
  7. Key-size comparisons describe how hard it is to break a key through cryptanalysis or brute-force; they say nothing about a scenario where the key has already been stolen outright, since a stolen key requires no cryptanalysis at all – the attacker simply has it. What determines the scope of damage here is the chain of trust’s structure: every certificate anywhere that chains up to this root, through any number of intermediate CAs, is now falsifiable. An attacker holding the root’s private key can forge a valid signature for a certificate claiming to be any website at all, and every browser with that root in its trust store will accept it without complaint until the compromise is disclosed and the root is actively removed or revoked. 5.4.B

Sources

  1. [1] National Institute of Standards and Technology, Recommendation for Key Management: Part 1 – General, NIST Special Publication 800-57 Part 1 Revision 5, May 2020, Table 2 (“Comparable security strengths of symmetric block cipher and asymmetric-key algorithms”), p. 54.
  2. [2] National Institute of Standards and Technology, Recommendation for Transitioning the Use of Cryptographic Algorithms and Key Lengths, NIST Special Publication 800-131A, January 2011 – setting a transition deadline of the end of 2013, after which 80-bit-strength algorithms including 1024-bit RSA are disallowed for applying cryptographic protection.

5.5 Secure Web Application Design

There are two ways of constructing a software design: one way is to make it so simple that there are obviously no deficiencies, and the other way is to make it so complicated that there are no obvious deficiencies.

C. A. R. Hoare, 1980 ACM Turing Award Lecture

Motivation

Chapter 5.1 showed what SQL injection, XSS, and BOLA look like from the attacker’s side. This chapter is the defender’s answer, built as a discipline rather than a single fix. Three habits make up that discipline: treat input as hostile by default, keep a codebase secure by construction rather than by after-the-fact patching, and let automated testing find what those habits still missed before an attacker does.

AP Course Alignment

Unit 5: Securing Applications and Data · Topic 5.5 – Protecting Applications

  • 5.5.A Mitigate Risk Explain the secure-coding habits (least privilege in code, parameterized queries, secrets management, failing securely, dependency management) that prevent vulnerabilities by construction, and how automated testing (SAST, DAST) plus human penetration testing verify those habits actually held.
  • 5.5.B Mitigate Risk Explain what checking untrusted input actually protects – which interpreter is prevented from receiving syntax it would have obeyed – and where the check has to run, why an allowlist beats a denylist, and which downstream defenses validation cannot replace.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

Input Validation and Sanitization

Every application is fundamentally a machine that takes input and does something with it. Some fraction of everyone typing into a form is not filling out their name – they are trying to break the application.

Definition: Input validation

Checking that input conforms to what an application expects before it is used, as distinct from sanitization, which transforms accepted input to remove or neutralize dangerous content rather than rejecting it outright.

Two philosophies govern validation. A denylist tries to enumerate the bad things – reject any input containing SELECT, a <script> tag, a semicolon. It fails constantly, because attackers are endlessly creative at encoding or rephrasing an attack past a specific blocked pattern (SELECT becomes SeLeCt; a semicolon becomes its URL-encoded %3B). An allowlist flips the logic entirely: define exactly what valid input looks like – a ZIP code is five digits, a quantity is an integer between 1 and 99 – and reject anything that does not match. Allowlisting is dramatically more robust because it never has to anticipate every possible attack. It only has to know what correct looks like, and correctness is almost always a far smaller, better-defined space than every way to attack a field.

Validation performed only in the browser is worthless for security, because the browser is entirely under the attacker’s control. JavaScript can be disabled, the page can be edited with developer tools, or a crafted HTTP request can bypass the browser entirely with a tool like curl. The rule to memorize is unconditional – never trust client-side validation for security; it must be re-enforced on the server on every request, regardless of what the client claims it already checked.

Before validating at all, an application often must canonicalize input to a single, predictable form. ../etc/passwd and its URL-encoded form ..%2Fetc%2Fpasswd are the same logical string in two different representations. A validator that checks the raw, un-canonicalized value can be tricked into approving something malicious, because it never recognized what the string actually decodes to. The correct order is decode and normalize first, then validate the canonical form. Validating before decoding is a classic bypass technique that smuggles path-traversal or injection payloads directly past a naive filter.

Common Misconception

“Sanitizing input once when it’s received protects it everywhere it’s later used.” The correct encoding depends entirely on the output context – HTML, SQL, a shell command, JSON. A single upstream sanitization pass is not a substitute for context-specific output encoding applied at each point data is actually used.

SQL injection, XSS, and command injection all share the identical root failure: an application trusted that input was “just data.” It actually contained characters a downstream interpreter – a SQL engine, a browser, a shell – treats as code. Rigorous input validation closes exactly this gap at its source, before any of Chapter 5.1’s three attacks ever gets the chance to run.

Secure Coding Practices

Input validation stops one specific failure. Secure coding is the larger discipline: habits and defaults built into every line of code so vulnerabilities are prevented by construction rather than caught after the fact.

Least privilege in code extends Chapter 4.2’s principle beyond human accounts to the code itself. A database connection should use an account that can only read and write the specific tables it needs, never one with administrative rights over the entire database. A web server process should never run as root, so that a single exploited bug becomes a contained failure rather than a full system compromise. Parameterized queries are the single highest-leverage habit against SQL injection specifically. Compare "SELECT * FROM users WHERE name = '" + name + "'" – dangerous, since the attacker’s input becomes query syntax – to a query with name passed as a separate bound parameter, which is safe because the driver treats it strictly as data no matter what characters it contains. One habit, applied everywhere a query touches user input, eliminates the risk regardless of what validation happened upstream. That is exactly why it counts as defense in depth alongside validation, rather than a replacement for it.

Secrets do not belong in source code at all: a hardcoded password or API key lives forever in version-control history, visible to anyone with repository read access. It is trivially found by both automated scanners and human review. Loading secrets at runtime from a secrets manager or environment variable means rotating a compromised one requires no code change or redeployment. Failing securely means an unexpected exception in an authentication check must deny access by default, never accidentally grant it. What a failure reveals matters too: a verbose error page showing a stack trace or internal file path hands an attacker reconnaissance information, so production code should log details internally while showing only a generic message externally. Finally, dependency management recognizes that a vulnerability in any one of an application’s dozens or hundreds of third-party libraries becomes a vulnerability in the whole application, exactly as Chapter 4.3’s patch-gap reasoning predicts. Excellent original code sitting on a five-year-old unpatched library is not secure code.

Common Misconception

“Secure coding is just input validation done well.” Input validation is one practice among several; parameterized queries, secrets management, least privilege, and dependency management each close an entirely independent class of vulnerability that validation alone cannot touch.

Application Security Testing

Writing secure code is not the same as knowing your code is secure. Two complementary automated testing families answer that question from opposite directions.

Definition: SAST (static application security testing)

Analyzing source code without executing it, searching for known-dangerous patterns – a string-concatenated SQL query, a call to eval() on user-controllable input, a hardcoded secret. SAST catches these the moment code is written, often directly inside an editor or as a CI/CD gate before a pull request merges.

Because SAST never runs the program, it sees every code path, including rarely exercised error handlers a live test might never stumble into. It catches vulnerabilities at the cheapest point to fix, before deployment. Its cost is a high false-positive rate: a static tool cannot always tell whether a flagged string concatenation actually reaches a database call with attacker-controlled input, so it tends to over-flag. It also cannot see anything that only exists because of how the deployed system is configured.

Definition: DAST (dynamic application security testing)

Testing a running application from the outside as a black box – sending real HTTP requests, fuzzing form fields, manipulating cookies. It observes the responses for signs of a vulnerability, the way an actual attacker would.

DAST naturally catches runtime and configuration issues source code alone cannot reveal, and its findings carry a lower false-positive rate. If the scanner injected a payload and got a genuinely suspicious response back, something real is usually happening. Its cost is the mirror image of SAST’s – it needs a running, reachable instance of the application, so it necessarily happens later in the development cycle. It can only find what it actually tests: code paths its crawler never reaches stay invisible.

Why the two approaches complement rather than compete

SAST is cheap, early, and exhaustive over source code but blind to runtime behavior and prone to false alarms. DAST is realistic and low-noise but late, incomplete in coverage, and blind to the code that produced whatever it observes. A mature CI/CD pipeline runs SAST on every commit for fast developer feedback and DAST against a staging deployment before release as a realistic pre-flight check, then correlates the two. A finding confirmed by both tools is high-confidence. A SAST-only finding needs a human to check reachability, and a DAST-only finding needs a human to trace back to the responsible line of code.

Neither replaces a human penetration test: a skilled person who chains together multiple weak findings and reasons about business-logic flaws no automated tool can recognize. “Can a regular user manipulate this parameter to view another user’s invoice?” is exactly the BOLA question from Chapter 5.1, and it takes human judgment, not a scanner, to notice it applies here. Mature programs use SAST and DAST for continuous, high-volume coverage and reserve human penetration testing for periodic, deeper assurance ahead of major releases.

Common Misconception

“A clean SAST/DAST scan means the application has no vulnerabilities.” Both tools only find what they are specifically built to look for. Business-logic flaws and novel attack chains typically require human penetration testing to uncover at all.

Check Your Understanding

1. Which of the following BEST describes the application security principle of "secure by design"?

Secure by design means security is not added as an afterthought but is treated as a foundational requirement from the very start of the software development lifecycle, present in requirements, architecture, and design decisions before a line of code is written. Option B describes a late-stage practice that is reactive rather than designed-in. Option C describes one specific control (encryption at rest), not the overarching principle. Option D describes a reactive patch cycle, the opposite of proactive design.

2. A software company releases a new application in which all user accounts are created with minimal permissions by default, and stronger access must be explicitly granted by an administrator. Which application security principle does this BEST illustrate?

Security by default means the application ships in its most secure configuration without requiring the end user or administrator to harden it manually. Granting minimal permissions from account creation, the principle of least privilege as a default state, is the textbook example. Secure by design (A) refers to the development-phase practice of building security in from the start, not specifically to the runtime configuration. Defense in depth (B) refers to layering multiple independent controls. Vulnerability management (D) refers to identifying and remediating known flaws.

3. Which of the following development activities is MOST consistent with the secure by design principle?

Threat modeling during planning is a foundational secure-by-design activity, it forces developers to systematically identify assets, threats, and mitigations before architecture decisions are locked in. Option A is a useful quality gate but occurs at the end of development, not by design. Option B is purely reactive. Option D actively undermines security and is the antithesis of designing security in.

Review Questions

  1. Explain why an allowlist validator for a ZIP-code field is more robust against novel attack variants than a denylist that blocks specific known-bad substrings.
  2. An application validates a filename only in its raw, un-decoded form before checking for path-traversal sequences. Explain how this ordering mistake lets an attacker smuggle a malicious path past the filter, and identify the correct order of operations.
  3. Explain why parameterized queries are described as defense in depth alongside input validation, rather than a replacement for it.
  4. A SAST scanner flags a string-concatenated SQL query as a potential vulnerability, but a human reviewer determines the concatenated value never actually contains user input. Explain why this illustrates SAST’s characteristic tradeoff.
  5. Explain why neither SAST nor DAST would likely catch a BOLA vulnerability where an authenticated user can view another user’s invoice by changing a URL parameter, and explain what kind of testing would.
  6. Using the CI/CD pipeline diagram, explain why running DAST only, and skipping SAST entirely, would still leave a codebase without the cheapest and earliest opportunity this chapter describes to catch a vulnerability, even if DAST eventually caught the same issue later.
  7. A finding is confirmed independently by both a SAST scan on a commit and a DAST scan against staging. Explain why this combination is treated as higher-confidence than either finding alone, referencing each tool’s characteristic weakness from this chapter.

Answer Key
