  1. A denylist must anticipate every possible way an attacker might encode or rephrase a bad value, which is an effectively unbounded space attackers keep finding new members of. An allowlist instead only has to define what a valid five-digit ZIP code actually looks like – a small, well-defined space – and rejects everything else by default. A novel attack variant it has never seen still fails to match the pattern and is rejected automatically. 5.5.B
  2. Checking the raw, un-decoded filename lets an attacker submit an encoded path-traversal sequence, such as ..%2Fetc%2Fpasswd, that does not visibly match the filter’s raw-text check for ../ but decodes to exactly that traversal once the application later normalizes it. The correct order is to decode and canonicalize the input to its single predictable form first, then validate that canonical form. That way the filter inspects the value the application will actually act on. 5.5.B
  3. Parameterized queries eliminate the SQL-injection risk at the database layer regardless of what validation happened upstream, but they do nothing for XSS, path traversal, or command injection in the same request. Input validation catches malformed or suspicious input broadly across all of these classes before it is used anywhere. Each closes a different gap, so running both together is layered defense in depth, not two attempts at the same fix. 5.5.A
  4. This illustrates SAST’s high false-positive rate. Because SAST never executes the program, it cannot always determine whether a flagged pattern’s actual runtime inputs are attacker-controlled, so it tends to over-flag constructs that merely resemble a vulnerability. Human review is needed to confirm reachability before treating a finding as real. 5.5.A
  5. SAST only recognizes dangerous code patterns in source, and correctly-written authorization code that simply forgets to check object ownership contains no dangerous pattern to flag. DAST sends automated requests and looks for anomalous responses, but a BOLA request returns a normal-looking, well-formed response. It is only wrong because it belongs to someone else, a business-logic judgment no automated tool is positioned to make. A human penetration test, which reasons about business logic such as “can this parameter be manipulated to view another user’s data,” is the kind of testing that catches it. 5.5.A
  6. SAST runs on source code before the application is ever deployed, catching a vulnerability at the cheapest possible point to fix – before a pull request even merges. DAST requires a running, reachable instance of the application, so it necessarily happens later in the development cycle, after code has already progressed further through the pipeline. In a real organization, more work has potentially been built on top of the flawed code by then. Skipping SAST means giving up that early, low-cost catch even if DAST eventually finds the identical issue. The fix now competes with everything built on top of it in the meantime, and the cost of fixing a defect generally grows the later it is caught. 5.5.A
  7. SAST’s characteristic weakness is a high false-positive rate, since it cannot always confirm a flagged pattern is actually reachable with attacker-controlled input. DAST’s findings carry a lower false-positive rate but a narrower scope, since it can only find what it actually tests. When both tools independently flag the same issue through their very different methods – one reading source, the other sending live requests – the chance that both are wrong about the exact same finding for unrelated reasons is far lower than either tool being wrong alone. That is why convergent findings from both are treated as high-confidence. 5.5.A

5.6 Application Threat Auditing

Trust, but verify.

Russian proverb, popularized in English by Ronald Reagan

Motivation

Chapter 5.5 built defenses into the application itself. This final chapter of the course is about what happens once an attack slips past those defenses anyway. It covers how an application’s own logs reveal it, how data leaving the organization is caught in the act, and how scattered evidence becomes a defensible conclusion about what actually happened. It closes the same loop Chapter 4.4 opened for devices, applied here to applications and data. This is the final layer of the course, and as always we pair an attack with the specific control that answers it.

AP Course Alignment

Unit 5: Securing Applications and Data · Topic 5.6 – Detecting Attacks on Data and Applications

  • 5.6.A Detect Attacks Account for how an attack against stored data becomes visible at all – which record of the event exists, which system wrote it, and what an analyst must compare it against before it means anything.
  • 5.6.B Detect Attacks Choose the controls that will actually produce that evidence for a given application and a given threat, rather than assembling a collection of tools that all watch the same thing.
  • 5.6.C Detect Attacks Judge a detection method on what it can and cannot see, the noise it generates per true finding, how quickly it fires, what it costs to operate, and whether an adversary can disable it.
  • 5.6.D Detect Attacks Determine whether a file is the file it should be by computing its digest and comparing that against a trusted reference, and recognize when the reference is not trustworthy.
  • 5.6.E Detect Attacks Read a sequence of log records well enough to name the attack, assess whether it succeeded, and write the result as a report someone else can act on without repeating the analysis.

Objective codes (e.g., 1.1.A) are this book's own numbering, keyed to the topic number. Objective content is paraphrased from the AP Cybersecurity Course Framework (College Board, AP Career Kickstart).

Application Monitoring and Logging

Every login, form submission, and error an application handles can be recorded as a structured, timestamped log line. An attacker probing for valid usernames, hammering a login form, or calling an endpoint they should not reach touches the application in exactly the same way a legitimate user does. That means every one of those actions can leave a mark.

The pattern is the signal, not the event

08:42:01Z  192.168.10.44  jsmith  POST /login  401  "Invalid credentials"
08:42:03Z  192.168.10.44  jsmith  POST /login  401  "Invalid credentials"
08:42:05Z  192.168.10.44  jsmith  POST /login  200  "Login successful"

Seen once, this is unremarkable – people mistype passwords. Seen fifty times, from a single IP, across twenty usernames within two minutes, it is a brute-force attack identical in shape to the one detected in Chapter 4.4. A single log line rarely proves anything; the correlated pattern across many lines is what actually carries evidentiary weight.

Several recurring signatures recur across real incidents. Brute-force / credential stuffing shows up as a high volume of 401 or 403 responses to a login endpoint from one IP, targeting many usernames in a short window. SQL injection probes appear as URL parameters or body fields containing the SQL meta-characters from Chapter 5.1 – ', --, UNION SELECT – captured verbatim in a raw access log. Privilege escalation attempts show up as a series of 403 responses from an authenticated low-privilege account suddenly requesting /admin/ or a configuration endpoint. Error storms – a sudden spike in 500-series responses concentrated on one endpoint – can indicate active exploitation of a code vulnerability forcing the server into an unexpected state.

Security-relevant events must be logged at INFO severity or higher, since a misconfiguration that drops them creates a blind spot an attacker can exploit by deliberately staying quiet enough to avoid it. Because raw logs are high-volume – a busy server can produce thousands of lines per second – teams feed them into a SIEM, exactly as in Chapter 3.5. SIEM rules apply logic like “if one IP produces five or more 401s within sixty seconds, alert.” Logging is not complete protection on its own: it cannot capture traffic that bypasses the application entirely, such as a compromised insider reading the database directly. An attacker who achieves code execution will often try to erase logs to cover their tracks. That is exactly why logs should stream to a separate, append-only, remote aggregator in real time, so local deletion does not erase the forensic record.

Common Misconception

“If no alarm fired, nothing bad happened.” Alarms only fire on configured rules; a missing or misconfigured rule is a silent blind spot regardless of what actually occurred. Periodic manual log review remains necessary even with automated alerting in place.

Data Loss Prevention

An organization can defend its perimeter, patch its applications, and monitor its logs, and still lose its most valuable asset quietly: the data itself.

Definition: Data loss prevention (DLP)

Tools and processes that detect, and where possible block, sensitive data leaving an organization’s control without authorization – whether the cause is a malicious insider, a compromised account, or an honest employee’s mistake.

Two detection strategies work together. Content-based detection inspects the actual data leaving the organization for recognizable patterns – a credit-card number format, an SSN pattern, a document labeled “CONFIDENTIAL” in its metadata. It is precise when it fires, but blind to anything an attacker compresses or encrypts before sending, since the recognizable pattern simply disappears. Volume/behavior-based detection looks instead at the shape of data movement rather than its content: an account that normally transfers a few megabytes a day suddenly uploading two gigabytes to an unfamiliar destination is anomalous regardless of what that data actually contains. That is exactly what lets behavior-based detection catch encrypted exfiltration that content inspection cannot see at all.

A mature DLP program instruments multiple vantage points, since different exfiltration paths are visible from different places. Network DLP watches traffic at the perimeter. Endpoint DLP runs on individual devices and can catch a USB copy that never touches the network at all. Cloud/email DLP inspects uploads and attachments before they leave a managed environment. Because legitimate large transfers happen constantly – backup jobs, sanctioned partner data-sharing – effective DLP maintains an approved destination list, so an identical transfer volume is silent to a known-good partner and alarming to an unrecognized external domain. Building and tuning that list is most of the ongoing work of actually running a DLP program.

Why DLP is preventive, not merely forensic

Unlike a log reviewed after the fact, a well-integrated DLP system can act in real time: blocking an email carrying a flagged attachment before it sends, or quarantining a file upload before it completes. This makes DLP one of the few controls in this course that can stop a loss during the attempt rather than only detecting it afterward. An attacker aware of this will try to blend exfiltration into normal-looking traffic specifically to avoid triggering it.

Common Misconception

“A large data transfer is always suspicious.” Legitimate large transfers – backups, sanctioned partner data sharing – happen constantly. Effective DLP relies on an approved-destination list and account-specific baselines, not raw transfer size alone.

File Integrity Monitoring

Logs and DLP both watch data in motion; a complementary control watches whether the files themselves have quietly changed. File Integrity Monitoring (FIM) computes a cryptographic hash of every file that matters – binaries, configuration files, web application source – while the system is in a known-good state. It stores those hashes as a trusted baseline and periodically recomputes them to detect drift. Any mismatch means the file changed. If no authorized change ticket explains it, that mismatch is evidence of compromise – this is the hash-comparison idea from Chapter 5.3 applied directly to forensics rather than to passwords.

A hand-rolled hash baseline is instructive but does not scale to enterprise use, which is why purpose-built FIM tools exist. Tripwire, first released in 1992 by Gene Kim, then a Purdue graduate student, pioneered the model of a policy file defining watched paths plus a cryptographically signed database, preventing an attacker who gains root from simply editing the baseline to hide their tracks.[1] AIDE (Advanced Intrusion Detection Environment) is its open-source, GPL-licensed replacement.[2] The reference the hashes are compared against must itself be trustworthy. A baseline stored on the same compromised host an attacker already controls can simply be rewritten alongside the files it is meant to catch, which is why a signed, off-host baseline matters as much as the hashing itself.

Judging a Detection Method

Three controls have been introduced so far – log-based monitoring, DLP, and FIM – and none of them is a strictly better or worse option than the others. Judging any detection method means asking the same five questions: what can it actually see, how much noise does it generate per genuine finding, and how fast does it fire? What does it cost to operate, and can an attacker who compromises the host disable it?

Log-based monitoring sees almost anything the application is configured to record, but a busy server’s raw volume means untuned rules drown a real alert in noise. An attacker with code execution can delete local logs outright, unless they are streamed off-host in real time. DLP sees data only as it crosses a boundary, and can block a transfer before it completes – something neither logs nor FIM can do. Content-based DLP is blind to anything encrypted; volume-based DLP produces false positives until its baselines are tuned. FIM detects unauthorized file changes cheaply and precisely, but only after the fact – it blocks nothing. Its own baseline is as vulnerable to on-host tampering as any other file, unless stored off-host and signed.

No single method wins on every axis. A mature detection program layers all three specifically because each control’s blind spot is a different control’s strength.

Digital Evidence Analysis and Attack Classification

Detecting that something happened is only half of incident response. Once an alert fires, an analyst has to answer the harder question of what actually happened and what evidence supports that conclusion.

Definition: Attack classification

The process of collecting multiple indicators of compromise and reasoning about which attack category they collectively and most strongly support, rather than treating any single indicator as conclusive on its own.

An alert is a hypothesis, not a verdict, and this echoes Chapter 4.4’s IOC-scoring logic directly. A spike in failed logins suggests brute-force, but confirming it requires checking whether the failures come from one source targeting many usernames (brute-force) or many sources targeting one username (credential stuffing from a leaked password list). Those are two genuinely different attacks that a single 401-count alone cannot distinguish.

Evidence supporting a real response decision – or a legal proceeding – must be handled with a documented chain of custody: who collected it, when, how it was stored, and who has touched it since. An analyst who copies a log to a personal laptop and edits it “to make it more readable” has compromised that evidence – not necessarily because it was tampered with, but because no one can prove it was not. Proper practice works from read-only copies and hashes the original evidence at collection time, using exactly the hash-verification idea from Chapter 5.3. That way any later change is immediately detectable.

Industry frameworks like MITRE ATT&CK give analysts a shared vocabulary, so that “brute-force” means the identical specific thing across every team and report.[3] Without it, one analyst’s classification cannot be meaningfully compared or aggregated with another’s.

Attack classification is rarely a solo act. A junior analyst collects and documents evidence, a senior analyst reviews the classification and challenges weak reasoning before it becomes an official record, and the final classification determines which response playbook a team follows from there. Honestly communicating uncertainty (“the evidence most strongly supports phishing, but a malware component cannot be ruled out”) is the mark of a mature analyst, not a weakness in the analysis.

Common Misconception

“One suspicious indicator is enough to declare the attack type.” A single IOC is rarely conclusive on its own, exactly as in Chapter 4.4. Classification should weigh the full set of collected evidence, not the loudest single signal.

Check Your Understanding

1. Which of the following BEST describes the purpose of a file integrity monitoring (FIM) system?

FIM computes cryptographic hashes (e.g., SHA-256) of monitored files and stores them as a known-good baseline. On each scan cycle it recomputes hashes and alerts when any value differs from the baseline, revealing unauthorized modifications to data or configuration files, a core data-attack detection strategy.

2. A security analyst downloads a software package from a vendor's website. Before installation, the analyst wants to verify the package has not been tampered with during transit. Which action BEST achieves this?

Hash verification works because a cryptographic hash function produces a unique fixed-length digest for a given input. Any modification to the file, even a single bit, changes the digest. Comparing the computed hash to the vendor's published value confirms the file's integrity. If hashes match, the file is unaltered.

3. Which type of log is MOST directly useful for detecting unauthorized access attempts against a web application?

Web server access logs record each HTTP request including source IP address, HTTP method, requested URI, and response status code. This data directly surfaces patterns characteristic of application attacks (e.g., repeated 4xx errors, directory traversal strings in URIs, abnormally rapid request sequences from a single IP).

Review Questions

  1. A log shows two failed logins followed by a success for one account within five seconds. Explain why this pattern alone is not conclusive evidence of an attack, and describe what additional evidence would change that assessment.
  2. Explain why volume/behavior-based DLP detection can catch an exfiltration attempt that content-based detection completely misses, using an encrypted file transfer as your example.
  3. An analyst copies an incident’s log file to a personal laptop and edits formatting for readability before sharing it with a colleague. Explain why this compromises the evidence’s chain of custody even if the analyst made no substantive changes to its content.
  4. A SOC receives a single alert: one account exceeded a 401-response threshold. Using the attack-classification reasoning from this chapter, explain why this alone cannot distinguish brute-force from credential stuffing, and describe the specific evidence that would resolve the ambiguity.
  5. Explain why logs should stream to a remote, append-only aggregator in real time rather than being stored only locally on the server that generated them, connecting your answer to what an attacker with code execution typically tries to do next.
  6. An organization stores its file-integrity-monitoring baseline on the same server the baseline is meant to protect. Explain why an attacker who gains root on that server can defeat the entire control, and describe what property the baseline’s storage location must have to prevent this.
  7. Using the chain-of-custody diagram, explain why analysis happens on a working copy rather than on the hashed original, and what specific guarantee would be lost if an analyst skipped that step and ran their tools directly against the original evidence file.
  8. A junior analyst classifies an incident as phishing, and a senior analyst reviewing the same evidence says the classification most strongly supports phishing but cannot rule out a malware component. Explain why the senior analyst’s hedged framing reflects better practice than the junior analyst’s confident single-category call, using this chapter’s own standard for what makes a classification credible.
  9. Using the five-question framework for judging a detection method, compare FIM to DLP: name one axis on which FIM is the stronger control, and one axis on which DLP is the stronger control, citing what this chapter says about each.

Answer Key

  1. Two failed logins followed by a success is consistent with an ordinary user mistyping a password twice, which happens constantly and carries no attack implication by itself. It would become concerning evidence if the same pattern recurred across many usernames from one source in a short window (brute-force), or the same username were targeted from many different sources (credential stuffing). A single instance lacks the correlated volume that gives the pattern evidentiary weight. 5.6.A
  2. Content-based detection inspects the data itself for recognizable patterns like a credit-card number format, but an encrypted file’s contents look like random bytes with no such pattern to match. Content inspection sees nothing suspicious. Volume/behavior-based detection instead watches the shape of the transfer – an account that normally moves a few megabytes a day suddenly sending two gigabytes to an unfamiliar destination is anomalous regardless of what the encrypted payload actually contains. It flags the transfer purely on its behavioral signature. 5.6.B
  3. Chain of custody requires being able to prove evidence was not altered after collection, not merely that it was not altered in substance. Once a copy exists on an uncontrolled personal device and has been edited at all, no one can prove from the evidence itself that only formatting changed and not content. The provable guarantee is gone even if the actual change was harmless, which is why proper practice works from read-only copies and hashes the original at collection time instead. 5.6.E
  4. A high count of 401 responses from a single account could mean an attacker is guessing that one account’s password from many sources (credential stuffing). It could also mean the alert is actually describing brute-force targeting many accounts and this is just one of them. The raw count alone does not reveal whether the failures are concentrated on one source IP against many usernames, or distributed across many source IPs against this one username. Correlating the failed attempts by source IP and target username resolves the ambiguity: one IP hitting many usernames is brute-force, many IPs hitting one username is credential stuffing. 5.6.E
  5. An attacker who achieves code execution commonly tries to erase local logs to cover their tracks. If those logs exist only on the compromised server, that deletion succeeds completely and destroys the forensic record along with the evidence of the attack itself. Streaming logs to a separate, append-only, remote aggregator in real time means the attacker’s local deletion cannot reach the copies that already left the machine, preserving the record regardless of what happens to the original. 5.6.B
  6. File Integrity Monitoring only detects tampering by comparing current file hashes against a trusted baseline. If the baseline sits on the same host, an attacker with root can recompute and overwrite the stored baseline hashes to match their tampered files, and every subsequent check reports a clean match because the reference itself was falsified. The baseline’s storage must be off-host or otherwise outside the attacker’s reach once they have compromised the monitored server – for example, a separate, access-controlled system, or a cryptographically signed database like Tripwire’s that requires a separate credential to update. Compromising the monitored host alone is not sufficient to also rewrite the reference it is checked against. 5.6.D
  7. Analysis happens on a working copy so that whatever tools, searches, or annotations the analysis requires can never alter the original evidence. If something needs to be tried again differently, or a mistake is made while analyzing, the untouched original is still there to copy from again. If an analyst instead ran tools directly against the original, any tool that modifies timestamps, reformats content, or otherwise touches the file during analysis would break the provable guarantee that the evidence is unchanged since collection – the entire point of having hashed it in the first place. The loss is not necessarily that the content changes, but that no one could prove it did not. 5.6.E
  8. This chapter’s standard is that classification should weigh the full set of collected evidence rather than the loudest single signal, and that honestly communicating uncertainty is the mark of a mature analyst, not a weakness. A confident single-category call implies a level of certainty the underlying evidence may not actually support. Explicitly naming what remains unresolved instead gives whoever acts on the classification an accurate picture of the actual evidentiary strength – including the possibility that the response playbook may need to account for a scenario the primary classification does not cover. 5.6.E
  9. FIM is the stronger control on cost and noise: once a baseline exists, comparing hashes is cheap to run and generates almost no false positives, since a mismatch is either an authorized change or a real compromise, unlike volume-based DLP, which needs tuned baselines and an approved-destination list to avoid raising them. DLP is the stronger control on speed and prevention: a well-integrated DLP system can block a transfer while it is still happening, while FIM only detects a change that has already occurred and never prevents the write in the first place. 5.6.C

Sources

  1. [1] Gene H. Kim and Eugene H. Spafford, “The Design and Implementation of Tripwire: A File System Integrity Checker,” Purdue University Computer Science Technical Report CSD-TR-93-071 (1993); Tripwire, Inc., “The Past, Present, and Future of File Integrity Monitoring,” The State of Security.
  2. [2] “Advanced Intrusion Detection Environment (AIDE),” project homepage, aide.github.io.
  3. [3] MITRE Corporation, “MITRE ATT&CK®,” attack.mitre.org.
